<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For a smooth strictly convex planar billiard of positive curvature, a continuous foliation of a full grazing annulus by individually invariant essential curves forces an analytic boundary and a jointly analytic collar of smooth strictly convex caustics. The physical-collar rigidity theorem proved in the companion article then implies that the table is an ellipse.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Suppose a $T$-invariant open annulus $U\subset M$ contains $(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb Z)\times(0,\eta)$ for some $\eta>0$. Suppose there is a homeomorphism $$H:\mathbb S^1\times(0,1)\longrightarrow U$$ whose leaves are essential simple closed curves, each individually invariant under $T$. Then $\Omega$ is an ellipse: there are $c\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and a real symmetric positive-definite matrix $Q$ such that $$\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^2:(x-c)^TQ(x-c)<1\}.$$ Circles are included.
  - Explanation: An exact continuous invariant foliation of a full grazing annulus forces an elliptical table, including a circular table.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-nb-rigidity)): A bounded strictly convex planar domain with $C^\infty$ regular boundary of positive curvature and a continuous physical caustic collar is an ellipse.
  - Explanation: The companion rigidity result identifies an ellipse from a continuous physical caustic collar under the stated geometric hypotheses.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-collar-regularity)): Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the boundary is real analytic. There are $\lambda_0>0$ and a jointly real-analytic map $$\Gamma:\mathbb T\times[0,\lambda_0]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^2$$ such that $\Gamma(\cdot,0)$ parametrizes $\partial\Omega$, every curve $\Gamma(\cdot,\lambda)$ for $0<\lambda\le\lambda_0$ is a regular strictly convex caustic, and the restriction to $\mathbb T\times[0,\lambda_0)$ is a homeomorphism onto a relatively open collar of $\partial\Omega$ in $\overline\Omega$. Here real analyticity at the parameter endpoints means extension to a neighborhood.
  - Explanation: The phase foliation yields an analytic boundary and a jointly analytic family filling a physical caustic collar.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-geo-barrier)): For each fixed pair of leaves there is a continuous real argument $A(\alpha,\beta)$ of $(x_0,x_1)$, periodic under simultaneous translation of $\alpha,\beta$ by $2\pi$. With $A_j=A(G_{b_1}^j(\alpha),G_{b_2}^j(\beta))$, it satisfies $$\label{geo:argument-step} -\frac{3\pi}{2}<A_{j+1}-A_j<\frac\pi2,$$ and, for every integer $m$, $$\label{geo:barrier-rule} A_j\le a_m:=\frac\pi2+\pi m \quad\Longrightarrow\quad A_{j+1}<a_m.$$ If $x_i$ and $x_k$ have opposite strict signs for some $i<k$, then $A_j$ cannot return arbitrarily close to $A_i$ as $j\to+\infty$.
  - Explanation: The separation argument has one-way barriers that exclude recurrent changes of strict separation sign.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-graph-collar)): Every leaf in $U$ is a graph $p=g_b(\theta)$. After reversing $b$ if necessary, $g_b$ is strictly decreasing in $b$, jointly continuous in $(\theta,b)$, and converges uniformly to $h$ as $b\downarrow0$. The induced angular lifts $$F_b=\theta_b\circ G_b\circ\theta_b^{-1}$$ are strictly ordered with $b$ and have positive translation numbers tending to zero at that end. Every sufficiently small positive rational $l/q$ occurs on exactly one leaf, and on that entire leaf $$\label{geo:rational-periodicity} F_b^q(\theta)=\theta+2\pi l.$$ Here rotation number means translation number divided by $2\pi$.
  - Explanation: Continuous invariant leaves become ordered graphs, and sufficiently small rational rotations have unique wholly periodic leaves.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-stationary-closure)): There is $\kappa>0$ with the following property. Let $q\ge1$ and $l$ be integers, and let $\theta_0,\ldots,\theta_q$ be a real chain such that $$0<\theta_{j+1}-\theta_j<\kappa,\qquad \theta_q=\theta_0+2\pi l,$$ and suppose it is stationary at all interior nodes: $$\label{geo:interior-stationarity} \mathcal{S}_2(\theta_{j-1},\theta_j) +\mathcal{S}_1(\theta_j,\theta_{j+1})=0, \qquad 1\le j<q.$$ Define the momenta, including the final one, by $$\label{geo:chain-momenta} p_j=-\mathcal{S}_1(\theta_j,\theta_{j+1})\quad(0\le j<q), \qquad p_q=\mathcal{S}_2(\theta_{q-1},\theta_q).$$ Then $\widetilde T(\theta_j,p_j)=(\theta_{j+1},p_{j+1})$ for every $0\le j<q$, and $p_q=p_0$. The chain therefore extends to a periodic orbit of rotation $l/q$, on the unique graph with that rotation. The action $\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}\mathcal{S}(\theta_j,\theta_{j+1})$ is constant along any $C^1$ family of such chains with fixed $q,l$.
  - Explanation: Interior stationarity and a small positive angular step force endpoint momentum closure and constant action along smooth families.
- **C7** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-analytic-boundary)): Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the support function $h$ and the boundary of the table are real analytic.
  - Explanation: The support function and boundary are analytic under the phase-foliation hypotheses.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-mesh-input)): Fix an integer $m\ge2$ and a finite bound $M$, and put $p=m+2$, $r_0=m+15$. There are $R,K,N$, depending only on $m,M$, with the following properties. For $0\le\sigma\le1$, $\\|f\\|_{r_0,\sigma}\le M$, and $q\ge N$, the action $D_f$ has an interior-stationary mesh with endpoints $X_0=x$, $X_q=x+2\pi$, of the form $$X_j(x)=x+2\pi j/q+w_j(x),\qquad w_0=w_q=0.$$ On the strip of width $\sigma'=(1-K/q^2)\sigma$, $$\label{ana:mesh-bounds} q^2\max_j\\|w_j\\|_{p,\sigma'}+ q^3\max_j\\|w_{j+1}-w_j\\|_{p,\sigma'}\le R.$$ The mesh is real symmetric and differentiable in smooth real input variations; its derivative in the norm on the left of <a href="#ana:mesh-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:mesh-bounds">[ana:mesh-bounds]</a> is bounded by $C\\|\dot f\\|_{r_0,\sigma}$. The corresponding difference bound holds for two inputs. On the real axis the mesh is pointwise unique in the ball with these scaled position and step bounds. Any fixed enlargement of that real uniqueness ball is allowed after increasing $N$.
  - Explanation: Arbitrary bounded normalized inputs admit stationary meshes with controlled scaled displacements and input dependence.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-row-input)): For fixed $m\ge2$, $M<\infty$, and $r_0=m+15$, there are $K,N$, independent of $0\le\sigma\le1$, such that for $\\|f\\|_{r_0,\sigma}\le M$ and $\|n\|\ge N$, $$\label{ana:row-derivative} (\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}C_n)_f(v)=[b_n(f)v]_n,$$ where $$\begin{aligned} \\|b_n(f)-1\\|_{m,(1-K/n^2)\sigma}&\le K/\|n\|, \label{ana:row-bound}\\ \\|b_n(f)-b_n(\widehat f)\\|_{m,(1-K/n^2)\sigma} &\le (K/\|n\|)\\|f-\widehat f\\|_{r_0,\sigma}. \label{ana:row-difference} \end{aligned}$$ The second estimate holds for two inputs with the same bound. The derivative identity holds for smooth real variations and for real-symmetric holomorphic variations with the indicated finite norms.
  - Explanation: The Fourier-row derivative is multiplication followed by coefficient selection, with estimates uniform in strip width.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-ana-vanishing)): For the smooth function $u$ in <a href="#ana:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:normalization">[ana:normalization]</a>, $C_n(u)=0$ for every sufficiently large $\|n\|$.
  - Explanation: The actual normalized boundary data satisfy all sufficiently high Fourier equations.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-ana-wiener)): Fix the bounds in Proposition <a href="#prop:row-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:row-input">9</a> with $m=2$. The row formula extends to a bounded Fourier operator $$A(f)=\Pi_H+R(f),\qquad \\|R(f)\\|\le C/N,$$ on each Wiener space of width $\sigma$. For two bounded inputs, $$\\|R(f)-R(\widehat f)\\| \le C\\|f-\widehat f\\|_{r_0,\sigma}.$$ For sufficiently large $N$, the restriction $A_H(f)$ to the high input modes is invertible with $\\|A_H(f)^{-1}\\|\le2$. The operators and this inverse preserve real Fourier symmetry. With higher fixed input bounds, the same matrix is bounded in $\|\cdot\|_{\sigma,a}$ whenever $m>a+1$.
  - Explanation: The high-mode Fourier derivative is an invertible small perturbation of the high-mode projection.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-ana-glancing-interface)): For the actual table, $D=D_u$ is real symmetric and holomorphic in a uniform neighborhood of the real diagonal. It is diagonally $2\pi$-periodic and antisymmetric, and satisfies $$\label{ana:glancing-expansion} D(x,x+b)=-\frac{b^3}{24}+O(b^5),\qquad D_1(x,x+b)=\frac{b^2}{8}+O(b^4),$$ locally uniformly with each fixed number of derivatives.
  - Explanation: The normalized action is holomorphic near grazing and has the stated universal leading expansion.
- **C13** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-joint)): There are positive numbers $s,d$, with $d<s$, and a function $V(y,t)$, holomorphic on $\{\|\Im y\|<2s,\ \|t\|<d\}$, such that $V-y$ is $2\pi$-periodic in $y$, $V$ is even in $t$, and $$V(\bar y,\bar t)=\overline{V(y,t)},\qquad V(y,0)=y.$$ Moreover $V_y$ is uniformly close to one, and $$D_1\bigl(V(y,t),V(y+t,t)\bigr) +D_2\bigl(V(y-t,t),V(y,t)\bigr)=0 \label{joint:stationary}$$ for $\|\Im y\|<s$, $\|t\|<d$. In particular $V_y>0$ on the real domain.
  - Explanation: A jointly holomorphic parametrization solves stationarity and is increasing on the real domain.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-joint-factorization)): The expressions $E$ and $a$ extend holomorphically across $t=0$, with $$E(V)(y,0)=\frac{(V')^2V''}{4},\qquad a(V)(y,0)=\frac{(V')^3}{4}. \label{joint:at-zero}$$ They are even in $t$ whenever $V$ is even, and $\langle E(V)\rangle=0$. The linearization in a variation $V'w$ is $$E_V(V'w)=\partial(a\partial w)+(Ew)'. \label{joint:linearization}$$ At an exact solution at a nonzero real parameter $t_0$, an assigned parameter jet $\dot V=V'z$ gives the total residual jet $$\dot E=\partial\left(a\partial z+\frac{a}{t_0}\right). \label{joint:parameter-jet}$$ Here the dot differentiates all parameter dependence, including the explicit shifts.
  - Explanation: The residual and coefficient extend across the grazing parameter; only the residual is asserted to have zero mean.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-joint-newton)): There are fixed constants $C_0,B_0$, depending only on the fixed analytic action bounds and candidate smallness bounds, for which the following holds. Suppose $$\\|E(V)\\|_{\mathcal D_0^1}\le\varepsilon<1, \qquad e^{-N\delta}\le\varepsilon, \qquad Q=1+N+\delta^{-1},$$ where $N\ge1$ is an integer. If $C_0Q^{B_0}\varepsilon$ is sufficiently small, there is a periodic, real-symmetric, even correction $\Delta V$ such that $$\begin{aligned} \max_{0\le j\le3}\\|\partial_y^j\Delta V\\|_{\mathcal D_{20}^0} &\le C_0Q^{B_0}\varepsilon, \label{joint:step-size}\\ \\|E(V+\Delta V)\\|_{\mathcal D_{20}^1} &\le C_0Q^{B_0}\varepsilon^2. \label{joint:step-error} \end{aligned}$$ In particular $B_0$ is independent of the radius $d$, the retained rational parameters, and the order of a formal initializer.
  - Explanation: A uniformly controlled Newton correction gives a quadratic residual bound under the stated smallness conditions.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-joint-compatibility)): Let $t_0=2\pi l/q\ne0$ be reduced, with $q\le N$ and $\|t_0\|<d-2\delta$. Under the hypotheses and polynomial smallness conditions of Proposition <a href="#joint:newton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:newton">15</a>, there are holomorphic periodic jets $w,\dot w$ on $Y_{10}$, real-symmetric in $y$, satisfying $$\begin{aligned} w,\dot w&=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon),\notag\\ E+\partial(a\partial w)&=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2), \label{joint:value-compatibility}\\ \bigl(E+\partial(a\partial w)\bigr)^{\displaystyle\cdot} &=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2). \label{joint:jet-compatibility} \end{aligned}$$ The dot in the last line uses the assigned jet $\dot w$ and differentiates every occurrence of $t$.
  - Explanation: Rational resonances admit compatible residual values and assigned first parameter jets up to quadratic error.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-joint-division)): Let $\nu\in\{1/2,1\}$, and suppose a mean-zero periodic function $F$ is holomorphic on $\mathcal D_{j_0}^\nu$, with norm at most $M$. Let the fixed integer buffers satisfy $b_{\mathrm{buf}}\ge j_0+1$ and $a_{\mathrm{buf}}\ge j_0$. Suppose, for $1\le \|n\|\le N$ and every divisor zero with $0<\|t_j\|<d-b_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta$, that $$\|F_n(t_j)\|\le\eta e^{-\|n\|(s-a_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta)}.$$ For the retained modes define $$\begin{aligned} R_n(t)&=\sum_{0<\|t_j\|<d-b_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta} F_n(t_j) \frac{\sin(n(t-t_j)/2)}{n(t-t_j)/2}, \label{joint:interpolation}\\ H_n(t)&=\frac{F_n(t)-R_n(t)}{d_n(t)}, \label{joint:quotient} \end{aligned}$$ and set $H_0=0$ and $H_n=0$ for $\|n\|>N$. The quotients are holomorphic for $\|t\|<d-(b_{\mathrm{buf}}+1)\delta$. For each fixed integer $J>\max(a_{\mathrm{buf}},j_0,b_{\mathrm{buf}}+1)$, $$\begin{aligned} \\|H\\|_{\mathcal D_J^{\nu-1/2}}&\le CQ^B(M+\eta),\notag\\ \\|\partial H-F\\|_{\mathcal D_J^\nu} &\le CQ^B\bigl(\eta+Me^{-N\delta}\bigr). \label{joint:division-bounds} \end{aligned}$$ Here $C,B$ depend on the fixed buffers, not on $N,d,\delta$. Fixed additional derivatives cost fixed additional powers after fixed domain losses. Real symmetry and evenness are preserved.
  - Explanation: Buffered Fourier division cancels retained resonances and controls the primitive and residual on smaller domains.
- **C18** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-physical-collar)): There exist $\lambda_0>0$ and a jointly real-analytic family $$\Gamma_\lambda:\mathbb{T}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^2, \qquad 0\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_0,$$ with the following properties. The zero leaf parametrizes $\partial\Omega$; each positive leaf is a smooth, simple, regular, strictly convex caustic contained in $\Omega$; and the map $$(\theta,b)\longmapsto\Gamma_{b\lambda_0}(\theta), \qquad (\theta,b)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,1),$$ is a homeomorphism onto a relatively open neighborhood of $\partial\Omega$ in $\overline\Omega$. Joint real analyticity here means extension to a neighborhood of the closed parameter cylinder.
  - Explanation: Analytic invariant line graphs yield nested strictly convex caustics filling a relatively open physical collar.

        "exact_quote": "Fix an integer $m\\ge2$ and a finite bound $M$, and put $p=m+2$, $r_0=m+15$. There are $R,K,N$, depending only on $m,M$, with the following properties. For $0\\le\\sigma\\le1$, $\\|f\\|_{r_0,\\sigma}\\le M$, and $q\\ge N$, the action $D_f$ has an interior-stationary mesh with endpoints $X_0=x$, $X_q=x+2\\pi$, of the form $$X_j(x)=x+2\\pi j/q+w_j(x),\\qquad w_0=w_q=0.$$ On the strip of width $\\sigma'=(1-K/q^2)\\sigma$, $$\\label{ana:mesh-bounds}\n q^2\\max_j\\|w_j\\|_{p,\\sigma'}+\n q^3\\max_j\\|w_{j+1}-w_j\\|_{p,\\sigma'}\\le R.$$ The mesh is real symmetric and differentiable in smooth real input variations; its derivative in the norm on the left of <a href=\"#ana:mesh-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ana:mesh-bounds\">[ana:mesh-bounds]</a> is bounded by $C\\|\\dot f\\|_{r_0,\\sigma}$. The corresponding difference bound holds for two inputs. On the real axis the mesh is pointwise unique in the ball with these scaled position and step bounds. Any fixed enlargement of that real uniqueness ball is allowed after increasing $N$.",
      "explanation": "Arbitrary bounded normalized inputs admit stationary meshes with controlled scaled displacements and input dependence.",

        "anchor": "stmt-ana-wiener",
        "exact_quote": "Fix the bounds in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:row-input\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:row-input\">9</a> with $m=2$. The row formula extends to a bounded Fourier operator $$A(f)=\\Pi_H+R(f),\\qquad\n \\|R(f)\\|\\le C/N,$$ on each Wiener space of width $\\sigma$. For two bounded inputs, $$\\|R(f)-R(\\widehat f)\\|\n \\le C\\|f-\\widehat f\\|_{r_0,\\sigma}.$$ For sufficiently large $N$, the restriction $A_H(f)$ to the high input modes is invertible with $\\|A_H(f)^{-1}\\|\\le2$. The operators and this inverse preserve real Fourier symmetry. With higher fixed input bounds, the same matrix is bounded in $|\\cdot|_{\\sigma,a}$ whenever $m>a+1$.",

      },

        "anchor": "stmt-ana-glancing-interface",
        "exact_quote": "For the actual table, $D=D_u$ is real symmetric and holomorphic in a uniform neighborhood of the real diagonal.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-joint",
        "exact_quote": "There are positive numbers $s,d$, with $d<s$, and a function $V(y,t)$, holomorphic on $\\{|\\Im y|<2s,\\ |t|<d\\}$, such that $V-y$ is $2\\pi$-periodic in $y$, $V$ is even in $t$, and $$V(\\bar y,\\bar t)=\\overline{V(y,t)},\\qquad V(y,0)=y.$$ Moreover $V_y$ is uniformly close to one, and $$D_1\\bigl(V(y,t),V(y+t,t)\\bigr)\n +D_2\\bigl(V(y-t,t),V(y,t)\\bigr)=0\n \\label{joint:stationary}$$ for $|\\Im y|<s$, $|t|<d$. In particular $V_y>0$ on the real domain.",
        "source_id": "PM-147.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-joint",
          "exact_quote": "There are positive numbers $s,d$, with $d<s$, and a function $V(y,t)$, holomorphic on $\\{|\\Im y|<2s,\\ |t|<d\\}$, such that $V-y$ is $2\\pi$-periodic in $y$, $V$ is even in $t$, and $$V(\\bar y,\\bar t)=\\overline{V(y,t)},\\qquad V(y,0)=y.$$ Moreover $V_y$ is uniformly close to one, and $$D_1\\bigl(V(y,t),V(y+t,t)\\bigr)\n +D_2\\bigl(V(y-t,t),V(y,t)\\bigr)=0\n \\label{joint:stationary}$$ for $|\\Im y|<s$, $|t|<d$. In particular $V_y>0$ on the real domain.",
          "source_id": "PM-147.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There are positive numbers $s,d$, with $d<s$, and a function $V(y,t)$, holomorphic on $\\{|\\Im y|<2s,\\ |t|<d\\}$, such that $V-y$ is $2\\pi$-periodic in $y$, $V$ is even in $t$, and $$V(\\bar y,\\bar t)=\\overline{V(y,t)},\\qquad V(y,0)=y.$$ Moreover $V_y$ is uniformly close to one, and $$D_1\\bigl(V(y,t),V(y+t,t)\\bigr)\n +D_2\\bigl(V(y-t,t),V(y,t)\\bigr)=0\n \\label{joint:stationary}$$ for $|\\Im y|<s$, $|t|<d$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-joint-factorization",
        "exact_quote": "The expressions $E$ and $a$ extend holomorphically across $t=0$, with $$E(V)(y,0)=\\frac{(V')^2V''}{4},\\qquad\n a(V)(y,0)=\\frac{(V')^3}{4}.\n \\label{joint:at-zero}$$ They are even in $t$ whenever $V$ is even, and $\\langle E(V)\\rangle=0$. The linearization in a variation $V'w$ is $$E_V(V'w)=\\partial(a\\partial w)+(Ew)'.\n \\label{joint:linearization}$$ At an exact solution at a nonzero real parameter $t_0$, an assigned parameter jet $\\dot V=V'z$ gives the total residual jet $$\\dot E=\\partial\\left(a\\partial z+\\frac{a}{t_0}\\right).\n \\label{joint:parameter-jet}$$ Here the dot differentiates all parameter dependence, including the explicit shifts.",

      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-joint-newton",
        "exact_quote": "There are fixed constants $C_0,B_0$, depending only on the fixed analytic action bounds and candidate smallness bounds, for which the following holds. Suppose $$\\|E(V)\\|_{\\mathcal D_0^1}\\le\\varepsilon<1,\n \\qquad e^{-N\\delta}\\le\\varepsilon,\n \\qquad Q=1+N+\\delta^{-1},$$ where $N\\ge1$ is an integer. If $C_0Q^{B_0}\\varepsilon$ is sufficiently small, there is a periodic, real-symmetric, even correction $\\Delta V$ such that $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\max_{0\\le j\\le3}\\|\\partial_y^j\\Delta V\\|_{\\mathcal D_{20}^0}\n   &\\le C_0Q^{B_0}\\varepsilon,\n   \\label{joint:step-size}\\\\\n \\|E(V+\\Delta V)\\|_{\\mathcal D_{20}^1}\n   &\\le C_0Q^{B_0}\\varepsilon^2.\n   \\label{joint:step-error}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In particular $B_0$ is independent of the radius $d$, the retained rational parameters, and the order of a formal initializer.",
        "source_id": "PM-147.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-joint-newton",
          "exact_quote": "There are fixed constants $C_0,B_0$, depending only on the fixed analytic action bounds and candidate smallness bounds, for which the following holds.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-joint-compatibility",
        "exact_quote": "Let $t_0=2\\pi l/q\\ne0$ be reduced, with $q\\le N$ and $|t_0|<d-2\\delta$. Under the hypotheses and polynomial smallness conditions of Proposition <a href=\"#joint:newton\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"joint:newton\">15</a>, there are holomorphic periodic jets $w,\\dot w$ on $Y_{10}$, real-symmetric in $y$, satisfying $$\\begin{aligned}\n w,\\dot w&=O_{\\mathrm p}(\\varepsilon),\\notag\\\\\n E+\\partial(a\\partial w)&=O_{\\mathrm p}(\\varepsilon^2),\n \\label{joint:value-compatibility}\\\\\n \\bigl(E+\\partial(a\\partial w)\\bigr)^{\\displaystyle\\cdot}\n   &=O_{\\mathrm p}(\\varepsilon^2).\n \\label{joint:jet-compatibility}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The dot in the last line uses the assigned jet $\\dot w$ and differentiates every occurrence of $t$.",
        "source_id": "PM-147.1"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Smooth convex caustic** ([source](#pm-h-4)): A *smooth convex caustic* is a $C^\infty$ simple closed regular curve in $\Omega$, bounding a strictly convex set, such that a billiard segment tangent to it remains tangent after reflection at either endpoint.
- **Continuous physical caustic collar** ([source](#pm-h-4)): A *continuous physical caustic collar* is a relatively open set $A\subset\overline\Omega$, containing $\partial\Omega$, together with a homeomorphism $$H_0:\mathbb S^1\times[0,1)\longrightarrow A$$ whose zero leaf is $\partial\Omega$ and whose positive leaves are smooth convex caustics.
- **Angular circle** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Write $\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb Z$ for the angular circle.
- **Real symmetry** ([source](#pm-h-6)): A periodic holomorphic function is *real symmetric* if $f(\bar z)=\overline{f(z)}$; the same convention applies in two variables.
- **Normalized action** ([source](#ana:normalization)): The normalized action is $$D_u(X,Y)=c_*^{-1}\left\{ \mathcal{S}(\phi(X),\phi(Y))- \int_{\phi(X)}^{\phi(Y)}h(\eta)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\right\}.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-prop-graph-collar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-graph-collar): Twist barriers, finite-area recurrence, rational passage, and continuity. -> Ordered graphs and unique wholly periodic small rational leaves.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-stationary-closure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stationary-closure): Generating relations and invariance of a single graph. -> Endpoint momentum closure and constant stationary action.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-thm-analytic-boundary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ana-wiener-2): Fourier truncation, bounded high-mode inversion, shrinking-strip Newton iteration, and real uniqueness. -> Real-analytic boundary and support function.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-joint) / [proof](#proof-stmt-joint-division-2): Formal initialization, compatible rational jets, buffered Fourier divisions, and analytic Newton convergence. -> Joint holomorphic stationarity parametrization.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-prop-physical-collar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-physical-collar): Support envelopes, positive curvature radius, strict inward motion, and nesting. -> A physical collar of smooth strictly convex caustics.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-physical-collar-3): Apply the companion physical-collar rigidity theorem. -> The table is an ellipse.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-rigidity**: The continuous invariant grazing foliation forces the billiard table to be an ellipse, including the circular case.
- **I-area**: The billiard map preserves the oriented-line area form; the positive area measure is finite and has full support.
- **I-rational**: Each phase leaf is an ordered graph, and each sufficiently small positive rational rotation belongs to a unique wholly periodic graph.
- **I-residual**: The normalized residual extends holomorphically across the grazing parameter, has zero angular mean, and is even when the candidate is even.
- **I-physical**: The jointly analytic family starts at the table boundary and its positive leaves are interior strictly convex caustics filling a physical collar.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-gaps**: Infer ellipticity from a Cantor family of grazing invariant curves. -> A family with gaps does not supply the full annular foliation needed for rational graph uniqueness and stationary closure.
- **A-approximation**: Infer exact rigidity from near-invariant trajectories or a finite numerical sample. -> Approximate invariance does not establish individual invariance of every essential leaf or the exact stationary equations.
- **A-curvature**: Apply the conclusion to tables with vanishing curvature or nonsmooth boundaries. -> The support-coordinate regularity and strict twist signs used by the proof require the stated positive curvature and smoothness.
- **A-general-twist**: Apply ellipse rigidity to an arbitrary area-preserving twist map. -> Area preservation and twist do not alone provide the billiard support geometry, physical caustics, and physical-collar rigidity theorem.
- **A-standalone**: Treat the passage from phase leaves to a physical collar as a standalone proof of ellipse rigidity without its companion inputs. -> The argument imports stationary-mesh and Fourier-row estimates, buffered division, and the final physical-collar rigidity theorem.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The source proves regularity and rigidity from a continuous foliation over an entire grazing annulus. Its analytic constructions use infinitely many Fourier equations and convergence on shrinking complex domains, with sufficiently large thresholds and sufficiently small radii rather than explicit effective bounds. Stationary finite meshes and finite formal initializers are intermediate steps, not a finite decision procedure for the foliation hypotheses or the ellipse conclusion. A finite source-derived certification algorithm is ABSENT.

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The rigidity conclusion requires a bounded strictly convex planar table with a smooth regular boundary of positive curvature. Blocked use: ['Polygonal or nonsmooth tables.', 'Tables with vanishing curvature.', 'Higher-dimensional billiards.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): The invariant annulus must contain a full grazing strip, and its essential leaves must each be individually invariant. Blocked use: ['A collection of sampled invariant trajectories.', 'An invariant annulus without an invariant foliation.', 'A map that permutes leaves instead of preserving each leaf.']
- **L3** (counterexample_scope): A family of invariant curves with gaps does not supply the full foliation hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Inferring ellipticity from a Cantor family of near-boundary invariant curves.']
- **L4** (approximation): An asymptotic grazing integral with a flat error does not establish the exact invariant foliation required for rigidity. Blocked use: ['Replacing exact invariance by asymptotic invariance.', 'Using finite-resolution orbit agreement as the rigidity hypothesis.']
- **L5** (edge_case): The foliation can be confined to an arbitrarily thin grazing annulus; global phase-cylinder integrability is not a conclusion. Blocked use: ['Extending the constructed collar to the entire phase cylinder.']
- **L6** (edge_case): Circles are included in the elliptic conclusion. Blocked use: ['Interpreting the conclusion as excluding circular tables.']
- **L7** (formal_scope): The ellipse conclusion invokes physical-collar rigidity from the companion article after the physical collar has been constructed. Blocked use: ['Presenting the ellipse conclusion as independent of the companion rigidity theorem.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The stationary-mesh estimates require bounded input regularity and sufficiently large mesh denominators. Blocked use: ['Applying the mesh estimates to arbitrary rough inputs.', 'Assuming the estimates apply to every denominator.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): The joint analytic construction requires rational compatibility for both residual values and first parameter jets. Blocked use: ['Omitting first parameter jets when justifying the second Fourier division.']
- **L10** (nonconstructive): The analytic constructions depend on sufficiently large thresholds and sufficiently small radii; an executable finite recognition procedure with certified input tolerances is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Certifying a full continuous foliation from finitely many trajectories.', 'Claiming numerical complexity or noise tolerance for recognizing an ellipse.']
- **L11** (formal_scope): Arbitrary trial inputs for the normalized action need not themselves represent billiard tables. Blocked use: ['Treating every trial action as a physical billiard table.']
- **L12** (nonconstructive): Initialization fixes a finite order before shrinking the parameter radius; no bound uniform over initialization orders is asserted. Blocked use: ['Claiming an initializer estimate uniform over all orders.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For a smooth strictly convex planar billiard of positive curvature, a continuous foliation of a full grazing annulus by individually invariant essential curves forces an analytic boundary and a jointly analytic collar of smooth strictly convex caustics. The physical-collar rigidity theorem proved in the companion article then implies that the table is an ellipse.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^2$ be a bounded strictly convex open set whose boundary is a $C^\infty$ simple closed regular curve with positive curvature. Orient the boundary counterclockwise, write its arclength coordinate as $s\in\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb Z$, and let $\delta\in(0,\pi)$ be the angle of the outgoing velocity measured counterclockwise from the positive tangent. Reflection at a boundary point with outward unit normal $n$ sends an incoming velocity $v$ to $v-2(v\cdot n)n$. The resulting billiard map $T$ acts on $$M=(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb Z)\times(0,\pi),$$ and $\delta\downarrow0$ is the grazing end considered below. Write $\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb Z$ for the angular circle.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Phase-foliation rigidity). *Suppose a $T$-invariant open annulus $U\subset M$ contains $(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb Z)\times(0,\eta)$ for some $\eta>0$. Suppose there is a homeomorphism $$H:\mathbb S^1\times(0,1)\longrightarrow U$$ whose leaves are essential simple closed curves, each individually invariant under $T$. Then $\Omega$ is an ellipse: there are $c\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and a real symmetric positive-definite matrix $Q$ such that $$\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^2:(x-c)^TQ(x-c)<1\}.$$ Circles are included.*

</div>

For an elliptical table, the confocal elliptic caustics give such a grazing foliation (Lazutkin 1973) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Lazutkin1973](#ref-Lazutkin1973)]; for a circular table, the corresponding caustics are concentric circles. Only continuity of the leaves and of their transverse dependence is assumed. The foliation need not extend to the rest of the phase cylinder, and the width $\eta$ may be arbitrarily small. The main issue is that a continuous essential phase curve need not initially be given as the tangent lines of a smooth convex caustic.

The complete companion article *Rigidity of Smooth Billiards with a Continuous Caustic Collar* (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)] supplies three analytic inputs and the final physical-collar rigidity theorem. Its stationary-mesh and Fourier-row estimates apply to arbitrary normalized actions; its buffered division lemma is an analytic estimate. Their precise hypotheses are recalled at the points of use. Here we establish the additional dynamical and geometric steps needed to apply them to a merely continuous phase foliation. In particular, we construct the physical collar before invoking the companion’s ellipse theorem.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and significance

The Birkhoff conjecture asks whether an integrable strictly convex planar billiard must be an ellipse. Birkhoff developed the convex billiard as a dynamical model (Birkhoff 1927) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Birkhoff1927](#ref-Birkhoff1927)]; Poritsky’s early printed formulation also studied compatible string constructions, under hypotheses stronger than a foliation alone (Poritsky 1950) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Poritsky1950](#ref-Poritsky1950)]. The distinction between a full foliation and a family with gaps is essential. Lazutkin’s theorem guarantees a Cantor family of near-boundary invariant curves for sufficiently smooth positively curved tables, rather than a full foliation (Lazutkin 1973) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Lazutkin1973](#ref-Lazutkin1973)]. Marvizi and Melrose constructed a grazing first integral modulo a flat error, not an exact invariant foliation (Marvizi and Melrose 1982) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[MarviziMelrose1982](#ref-MarviziMelrose1982)]. Positive curvature is substantive: Mather excluded grazing caustics when boundary curvature vanishes (Mather 1982) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Mather1982](#ref-Mather1982)].

Stronger or different integrability hypotheses give complementary rigidity results. Bialy proved that foliation of the entire phase cylinder by noncontractible invariant circles forces a circle (Bialy 1993) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Bialy1993](#ref-Bialy1993)]. Avila, De Simoi, and Kaloshin proved local rigidity near small-eccentricity ellipses (Avila, De Simoi, and Kaloshin 2016) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[ADK2016](#ref-ADK2016)], and Kaloshin and Sorrentino treated perturbations of ellipses of arbitrary eccentricity (Kaloshin and Sorrentino 2018) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KS2018](#ref-KS2018)]. Bialy and Mironov allowed a continuous phase foliation under central symmetry when the foliated region reaches the invariant four-periodic curve (Bialy and Mironov 2022) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BialyMironov2022](#ref-BialyMironov2022)]. Koval’s local strong result treats caustics at all rational rotations in an arbitrarily small interval above zero, under closeness to almost every ellipse and high-regularity bounds (Koval 2026) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Koval2026](#ref-Koval2026)]. These results impose geometric restrictions or require invariant curves extending beyond an arbitrarily thin grazing annulus.

Birkhoff’s invariant-curve theorem for twist maps explains why graphs are the natural intermediate objects (Arnaud 2009) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Arnaud2009](#ref-Arnaud2009)]. Here the initial leaves are only continuous essential curves, and the entire annulus is foliated. We therefore prove graphness directly from both billiard twist signs and finite-area recurrence. The full collar then supplies a wholly periodic graph at every sufficiently small rational rotation. The stationary actions of those graphs yield high-frequency equations for the boundary. First-order Fourier obstructions for rational caustics in perturbations of the circle were developed by Ramírez-Ros (2006) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[RamirezRos2006](#ref-RamirezRos2006)]; quantitative constraints near ellipses appear in Avila, De Simoi, and Kaloshin (2016) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[ADK2016](#ref-ADK2016)]. The analytic inversion here uses the companion’s arbitrary-input estimates without a perturbative assumption.

Together with the physical-collar rigidity theorem of (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)], Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the near-boundary Birkhoff conjecture positively in the continuous phase-foliation formulation above. The additional point established here is the passage from merely continuous invariant phase leaves to the smooth physical caustic hypothesis. This passage requires neither a symmetry assumption nor closeness to a circle or ellipse.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The regularity reduction

A *smooth convex caustic* is a $C^\infty$ simple closed regular curve in $\Omega$, bounding a strictly convex set, such that a billiard segment tangent to it remains tangent after reflection at either endpoint. A *continuous physical caustic collar* is a relatively open set $A\subset\overline\Omega$, containing $\partial\Omega$, together with a homeomorphism $$H_0:\mathbb S^1\times[0,1)\longrightarrow A$$ whose zero leaf is $\partial\Omega$ and whose positive leaves are smooth convex caustics. This is the hypothesis of the following companion theorem.

<a id="stmt-thm-nb-rigidity"></a>
<div id="thm:nb-rigidity" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Physical-collar rigidity, (OpenAI 2026, Definition 1.1 and Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]). *A bounded strictly convex planar domain with $C^\infty$ regular boundary of positive curvature and a continuous physical caustic collar is an ellipse.*

</div>

The core reduction proved below is stronger than the smoothness needed for this application.

<a id="stmt-thm-collar-regularity"></a>
<div id="thm:collar-regularity" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Regularity of a phase-foliated collar). *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the boundary is real analytic. There are $\lambda_0>0$ and a jointly real-analytic map $$\Gamma:\mathbb T\times[0,\lambda_0]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^2$$ such that $\Gamma(\cdot,0)$ parametrizes $\partial\Omega$, every curve $\Gamma(\cdot,\lambda)$ for $0<\lambda\le\lambda_0$ is a regular strictly convex caustic, and the restriction to $\mathbb T\times[0,\lambda_0)$ is a homeomorphism onto a relatively open collar of $\partial\Omega$ in $\overline\Omega$. Here real analyticity at the parameter endpoints means extension to a neighborhood.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:collar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:collar-regularity">3</a> uses the mesh and Fourier estimates from (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]. Its conclusion is proved directly from the phase foliation, without assuming the physical collar that appears in Theorem <a href="#thm:nb-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nb-rigidity">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Structure of the argument

The first step is topological and dynamical. In oriented-line coordinates the billiard has two strict twist inequalities. They impose one-way barriers on the lifted argument of successive angular separations. On an interval of equal rotation numbers, area recurrence can be applied on a suitable finite-measure part of the simultaneous cover of the product annulus. The resulting contradiction excludes vertical pairs. A separate rational-passage argument gives graph leaves densely without first assuming monotonicity of the rotation number. Limits then give graphs everywhere. Area recurrence also rules out rational plateaus, making each sufficiently small rational graph unique and wholly periodic. This graph argument is useful independently of the analytic estimates that follow.

The second step obtains boundary analyticity from stationary polygonal chains. Because the initial line lies on an invariant graph, a chain whose angle returns must also return its momentum. Its stationary action is therefore constant in the initial point. The associated high-frequency equations have derivative equal to the high-mode projection plus a small operator. A Newton construction on decreasing complex strips produces an analytic solution, and a real contraction identifies it with the original smooth boundary data.

The third step is a joint analytic construction in the angular and rotation variables. At rational resonances it uses both residual values and first parameter jets. The first jets control the coefficients left undetermined by one Fourier division, allowing a second division and a quadratic Newton step. The polynomial loss exponent is fixed before the order of formal initialization. These mechanisms come from the analytic machinery of (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]; we give the argument with its weaker geometric input explicit.

Finally, the invariant line graphs define analytic support functions with positive curvature radius. Their support decreases strictly to first order in the squared translation parameter, so their envelopes are nested convex caustics filling a physical collar. Figure <a href="#fig:phase-to-caustic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:phase-to-caustic">1</a> illustrates this final passage from an analytic line graph to its support envelope. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:collar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:collar-regularity">3</a> and makes Theorem <a href="#thm:nb-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nb-rigidity">2</a> applicable.

Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">2</a> proves the graph and rational-periodicity statements. Section <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">3</a> establishes analytic boundary regularity. Section <a href="#sec:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:joint">4</a> constructs the joint parametrization, and Section <a href="#sec:caustics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:caustics">5</a> proves the physical collar and concludes the rigidity argument.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Conventions.

All angular coordinates are real lifts of $2\pi$-periodic variables. Translation numbers are measured in radians; the corresponding rotation number is the translation number divided by $2\pi$. Constants may increase between estimates, with their dependence and order of choice specified when necessary. A periodic holomorphic function is *real symmetric* if $f(\bar z)=\overline{f(z)}$; the same convention applies in two variables.

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### From continuous leaves to periodic invariant graphs

We first extract the phase-space information needed by the analytic argument. This step uses the continuity of the foliation, both twist inequalities, and finite invariant area. It does not assume that a leaf is a graph or that it comes from a smooth caustic. Birkhoff’s invariant-curve graph theorem is classical for exact symplectic twist maps (Arnaud 2009) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Arnaud2009](#ref-Arnaud2009)]; the direct argument here also records the rational periodicity required by the subsequent stationary-action construction.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Oriented lines and the two twists

Translate the origin into $\Omega$. Write $$n_\theta=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),\qquad
 e_\theta=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta),\qquad
 h(\theta)=\max_{z\in\overline\Omega}z\cdot n_\theta.$$ The normal-angle parametrization of the boundary is smooth: strict convexity makes its normal map bijective, and positive curvature makes its derivative nonzero. Consequently <a id="geo:support"></a>$$\label{geo:support}
 \begin{gathered}
 h>0,\qquad r=h+h''>0,\\
 \gamma(\theta)=h(\theta)n_\theta+h'(\theta)e_\theta,
 \qquad \gamma'(\theta)=r(\theta)e_\theta.
 \end{gathered}$$ Represent a billiard state by its outgoing oriented line, with velocity $e_\theta$ and equation $z\cdot n_\theta=p$. The starting point is the backward intersection of that line with the boundary. Both intersections are transverse and uniquely determined, so this is a smooth change of coordinates onto $$\mathcal B=\{(\theta,p):\theta\in\mathbb{T},
                  \ -h(\theta+\pi)<p<h(\theta)\}.$$ We continue to denote the conjugated billiard map by $T$ and the given annulus by $U$.

<a id="geo:reflection"></a>
<a id="geo:twists"></a>
Choose the lift $\widetilde T$ for which the angular advance lies in $(0,2\pi)$. If $\gamma(\psi)$ is the forward collision point, reflection gives $$\label{geo:reflection}
 \begin{gathered}
 \theta'=\theta+2a,\qquad \psi=\theta+a,\qquad 0<a<\pi,\\
 p=h(\psi)\cos a-h'(\psi)\sin a,\qquad
 p'=h(\psi)\cos a+h'(\psi)\sin a.
 \end{gathered}$$ Indeed $e_\theta\cdot n_\psi=\sin a>0$, and reflection changes $e_\theta$ to $e_{\theta+2a}$. At fixed $\theta$, differentiation of $p$ with respect to $a$ gives $-r(\theta+a)\sin a<0$. At fixed $\theta'$, write $\psi=\theta'-a$; differentiation of $p'$ again gives $-r(\theta'-a)\sin a<0$. Writing $\theta_{-1}$ for the preceding lifted angle, we obtain $$\label{geo:twists}
 \frac{\partial\theta'}{\partial p}<0,
 \qquad \frac{\partial\theta_{-1}}{\partial p}>0.$$ Both derivatives hold the current lifted angle fixed. Thus increasing momentum decreases the next angle and increases the preceding angle.

The generating function in these line coordinates (Bialy and Mironov 2017) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BialyMironov2017](#ref-BialyMironov2017)] <a id="geo:generating"></a>$$\label{geo:generating}
 \mathcal{S}(\theta,\theta')
 =2h\!\left(\frac{\theta+\theta'}2\right)
       \sin\!\left(\frac{\theta'-\theta}2\right)$$ satisfies $p=-\mathcal{S}_1(\theta,\theta')$ and $p'=\mathcal{S}_2(\theta,\theta')$. Hence $T$ preserves $\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta$. The associated positive area measure on $\mathcal B$ is finite and has full support.

<a id="geo:uniform-collar"></a>
The hypothesis supplies a uniform upper strip in these coordinates. In fact, if the outgoing angle at the starting point is $\delta$, then the starting normal angle is $\theta-\delta$ and <a id="geo:starting-momentum"></a>$$\label{geo:starting-momentum}
 \begin{gathered}
 p=h(\theta-\delta)\cos\delta
                 +h'(\theta-\delta)\sin\delta,\\
 \frac{\partial p}{\partial\delta}
             =-r(\theta-\delta)\sin\delta<0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Compactness and the inclusion of all sufficiently small positive $\delta$ imply that, for some $\varepsilon_g>0$, $$\label{geo:uniform-collar}
 \{(\theta,p):h(\theta)-\varepsilon_g<p<h(\theta)\}\subset U.$$

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### A separation barrier for continuous leaves

<a id="geo:translation-bound"></a>
We record the elementary circle-dynamics facts that will also fix our rotation convention. If an increasing homeomorphism $G:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ commutes with translation by $2\pi$, its translation number satisfies $$\label{geo:translation-bound}
 \tau(G)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{G^j(\alpha)-\alpha}{j},
 \qquad
 |G^j(\alpha)-\alpha-j\tau(G)|\le2\pi
 \quad (j\in\mathbb Z).$$ For completeness, the displacements of each iterate oscillate by at most $2\pi$, by monotonicity. Their maxima are subadditive in the iteration index, and their minima are superadditive. Dividing by that index and using repeated blocks of each fixed length gives a common limit bracketed by the minimum and maximum divided by the index. Their oscillation bound then proves <a href="#geo:translation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:translation-bound">[geo:translation-bound]</a>. The negative-index case follows by starting a positive iterate at $G^j(\alpha)$. The same estimate, applied to a fixed large iterate, proves continuity of $\tau$ under locally uniform convergence of lifts. Also $\tau(G^q)=q\tau(G)$.

<a id="geo:translation-sign"></a>
One further consequence will be useful. For integers $q\ge1$ and $l$, $$\label{geo:translation-sign}
 \begin{aligned}
 \tau(G)>2\pi l/q&\ \Longrightarrow\
              G^q(\alpha)>\alpha+2\pi l\quad\text{for every }\alpha,\\
 \tau(G)<2\pi l/q&\ \Longrightarrow\
              G^q(\alpha)<\alpha+2\pi l\quad\text{for every }\alpha.
 \end{aligned}$$ A zero of the displacement difference would give a periodic orbit and force translation number $2\pi l/q$. In the absence of a zero, that continuous periodic difference has a constant strict sign. A uniformly negative difference forces $q\tau(G)-2\pi l<0$ by iteration, and a uniformly positive one forces the reverse inequality. This proves <a href="#geo:translation-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:translation-sign">[geo:translation-sign]</a>.

Use $b\in(0,1)$ for the transverse parameter in the given homeomorphism. A constant-small-$\delta$ circle lies in $U$ and has winding one in the phase cylinder. It is non-null in $U$ and, as an embedded essential circle of the annulus, represents a primitive generator of $H_1(U;\mathbb Z)$. Each product leaf represents that generator up to a common orientation choice. The normal map of the positively curved boundary has degree one, so the leaf’s starting normal angle also has degree one. The line angle is the starting normal angle plus the globally defined real angle $\delta\in(0,\pi)$, so it has the same degree. Lift the foliation to continuous parametrizations <a id="geo:leaf-lifts"></a>$$\label{geo:leaf-lifts}
 Z_b(\alpha)=(\theta_b(\alpha),p_b(\alpha)),\qquad
 Z_b(\alpha+2\pi)=Z_b(\alpha)+(2\pi,0).$$ Each $Z_b$ parametrizes the full inverse image of its leaf injectively. Individual invariance therefore defines a homeomorphism $G_b$ of $\mathbb{R}$ by $$\widetilde T Z_b=Z_b\circ G_b.$$ The deck relations for $Z_b$ and $\widetilde T$ give $G_b(\alpha+2\pi)=G_b(\alpha)+2\pi$. A decreasing homeomorphism cannot satisfy this relation, so $G_b$ is increasing. The inverses of the lifted foliation charts are continuous, so $G_b$ depends continuously on $b$. Set $\tau(b)=\tau(G_b)$. It is continuous by <a href="#geo:translation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:translation-bound">[geo:translation-bound]</a>; because $\theta_b(\alpha)-\alpha$ is bounded, it is also the limiting angular advance per iterate.

<a id="geo:opposite-signs"></a>
Fix two leaves. For distinct leaves use pairs $(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^2$; for a single leaf use the connected strip $\alpha<\beta\le\alpha+2\pi$. Define <a id="geo:separation"></a>$$\label{geo:separation}
 x_j=\theta_{b_2}(G_{b_2}^j(\beta))
                  -\theta_{b_1}(G_{b_1}^j(\alpha)).$$ If $x_j=0$, the two momenta are distinct, by simplicity and disjointness of leaves. The period-separated pair in the single-leaf strip has $x_j=2\pi$, so is not an exception. The two inequalities in <a href="#geo:twists" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:twists">[geo:twists]</a> give $$\label{geo:opposite-signs}
 x_j=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x_{j-1}x_{j+1}<0.$$ In particular the vector $(x_j,x_{j+1})$ never vanishes.

<a id="stmt-geo-barrier"></a>
<div id="geo:barrier" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:argument-step"></a>
<a id="geo:barrier-rule"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Separation barrier). *For each fixed pair of leaves there is a continuous real argument $A(\alpha,\beta)$ of $(x_0,x_1)$, periodic under simultaneous translation of $\alpha,\beta$ by $2\pi$. With $A_j=A(G_{b_1}^j(\alpha),G_{b_2}^j(\beta))$, it satisfies $$\label{geo:argument-step}
 -\frac{3\pi}{2}<A_{j+1}-A_j<\frac\pi2,$$ and, for every integer $m$, $$\label{geo:barrier-rule}
 A_j\le a_m:=\frac\pi2+\pi m
       \quad\Longrightarrow\quad A_{j+1}<a_m.$$ If $x_i$ and $x_k$ have opposite strict signs for some $i<k$, then $A_j$ cannot return arbitrarily close to $A_i$ as $j\to+\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-barrier"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-barrier</summary>

*Proof.* The pair plane and pair strip are simply connected, so the nonvanishing vector has a continuous real argument. For a single leaf normalize it to $\pi/4$ on $\beta=\alpha+2\pi$. For distinct leaves, both entries equal $\beta-\alpha+O(1)$, uniformly in the pair variables, so normalize the argument to $(0,\pi/2)$ on the connected region where $\beta-\alpha$ is sufficiently large. Either normalization implies simultaneous periodicity: the difference after a simultaneous translation is a continuous integer multiple of $2\pi$ and vanishes in the normalizing region.

Consecutive vectors have the form $(u,v),(v,w)$. A positive quarter-turn relation $(v,w)=c(-v,u)$, $c>0$, would force $v=0$ and $u,w$ to have the same sign, contrary to <a href="#geo:opposite-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:opposite-signs">[geo:opposite-signs]</a>. Thus $A_1-A_0$ avoids $\pi/2$ modulo $2\pi$. Connectedness, together with the edge normalization or the large-separation normalization, puts it in $(-3\pi/2,\pi/2)$. Applying this to the iterated pair proves <a href="#geo:argument-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:argument-step">[geo:argument-step]</a>.

If $A_j\le a_m\le A_{j+1}$, that bound gives $$a_m-\pi/2<A_j\le a_m\le A_{j+1}<a_m+\pi/2.$$ On these two intervals $\sin A_j$ is nonzero with sign $(-1)^m$, whereas $\cos A_{j+1}$ is either zero or has the opposite sign. This is impossible: both have the sign, and the same zero condition, of $x_{j+1}$. This proves <a href="#geo:barrier-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:barrier-rule">[geo:barrier-rule]</a>.

Finally, if $x_i\ne0$, then $A_i$ lies strictly between two consecutive barriers. All subsequent arguments stay below the upper one. If $x_k$ has the opposite sign, $A_k$ cannot remain in the interval between those barriers, so lies strictly below its lower endpoint. All later arguments remain below that endpoint, a positive distance from $A_i$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Graphs, recurrence, and rational rotation

<a id="stmt-prop-graph-collar"></a>
<div id="prop:graph-collar" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:rational-periodicity"></a>
**Proposition 5**. *Every leaf in $U$ is a graph $p=g_b(\theta)$. After reversing $b$ if necessary, $g_b$ is strictly decreasing in $b$, jointly continuous in $(\theta,b)$, and converges uniformly to $h$ as $b\downarrow0$. The induced angular lifts $$F_b=\theta_b\circ G_b\circ\theta_b^{-1}$$ are strictly ordered with $b$ and have positive translation numbers tending to zero at that end. Every sufficiently small positive rational $l/q$ occurs on exactly one leaf, and on that entire leaf $$\label{geo:rational-periodicity}
 F_b^q(\theta)=\theta+2\pi l.$$ Here rotation number means translation number divided by $2\pi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-graph-collar"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-graph-collar</summary>

*Proof.* *Periodic leaves are graphs.* Suppose first that $G_b^q=\mathrm{id}+2\pi l$ on a whole leaf. For its pair strip, simultaneous periodicity gives $A_q=A_0$. If $x_0=0$, then $A_0$ is a barrier, and <a href="#geo:barrier-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:barrier-rule">[geo:barrier-rule]</a> makes $A_q<A_0$, a contradiction. Thus $x_0$ never vanishes on the connected strip. It equals $2\pi$ on its upper edge, so is everywhere positive. This says precisely that $\theta_b$ is strictly increasing.

*Constant-translation intervals also consist of graphs.* Let $b$ be interior to an interval on which $\tau$ is constant. If $\theta_b$ were not strictly increasing, its degree-one property and continuity would give an ordered same-leaf pair with $x_0=0$. By <a href="#geo:opposite-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:opposite-signs">[geo:opposite-signs]</a>, $x_{-1}$ and $x_1$ have opposite strict signs. Start instead with that preceding pair and perturb it slightly. We obtain a nonempty open set $O$ of pairs on distinct leaves for which $x_0x_2<0$. We can take the two leaf parameters in disjoint relatively compact intervals $I_1,I_2$ inside the constant-translation interval, and take $|\beta-\alpha|$ bounded on $O$.

Consider the cover of $H(\mathbb{T}\times I_1)\times H(\mathbb{T}\times I_2)$ obtained from $(\mathbb{R}\times I_1)\times(\mathbb{R}\times I_2)$ by identifying only $$(\alpha,b_1,\beta,b_2)
       \sim(\alpha+2\pi,b_1,\beta+2\pi,b_2).$$ The pair dynamics $K$ induced by $(G_{b_1},G_{b_2})$ preserves the measure lifted from product billiard area. This measure is defined by integrating counting measure on the covering fibers against product area; $K$ bijects the fibers over the area-preserving base dynamics. It has full support, and any region of bounded $|\beta-\alpha|$ has finite measure, since it meets uniformly finitely many sheets over each base point. This construction requires no differentiability or absolute continuity of $H$.

Equality of the two translation numbers and <a href="#geo:translation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:translation-bound">[geo:translation-bound]</a> imply, for every $j\in\mathbb Z$, <a id="geo:relative-bound"></a>$$\label{geo:relative-bound}
 \left|(G_{b_2}^j(\beta)-G_{b_1}^j(\alpha))
                         -(\beta-\alpha)\right|\le4\pi.$$ Consequently the saturation $\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb Z}K^j(O)$ is an invariant finite-measure set. Poincaré recurrence gives recurrent points in $O$, indeed almost every point there is recurrent. One may obtain recurrence to a point from the usual measurable-set version by using a countable neighborhood base; the latter version follows because the forward images of a set of nonreturners are disjoint in finite measure.

For any such recurrent pair, the two leaf parameters remain fixed. Thus continuity of the argument for this fixed pair of leaves and simultaneous periodicity give $A_{j_k}\to A_0$ along a sequence $j_k\to\infty$. This contradicts Lemma <a href="#geo:barrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:barrier">4</a>, because $x_0x_2<0$. Hence $\theta_b$ is strictly increasing throughout every constant-translation interval.

*Graph leaves are dense, and then every leaf is a graph.* Take any open parameter interval $J$. If it contains a subinterval where $\tau$ is constant, the preceding argument supplies graph leaves. Otherwise choose a rational multiple $r_*=2\pi l/q$ strictly between two values of the continuous function $\tau$ on $J$. Travel along the parameter segment from the smaller value to the larger one, and let $c$ be the first position approached by values $\tau>r_*$. Continuity gives $\tau(c)=r_*$. Values strictly below $r_*$ also approach $c$: otherwise $\tau=r_*$ on an interval immediately before $c$, contrary to our case assumption. Taking limits in <a href="#geo:translation-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:translation-sign">[geo:translation-sign]</a> from these two strict sides gives $G_c^q=\mathrm{id}+2\pi l$. The periodic-leaf argument therefore supplies a graph in $J$. Notice that no monotonicity of $\tau$ has been used.

Graph parameters are thus dense. Continuity of the lifted foliation makes every $\theta_b$ nondecreasing. If a same-leaf ordered pair had $x_0=0$, its preceding and following pairs would still be ordered, because $G_b$ is increasing. Nondecrease would give $x_{-1},x_1\ge0$, contradicting <a href="#geo:opposite-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:opposite-signs">[geo:opposite-signs]</a>. Every $\theta_b$ is therefore strictly increasing, proving the graph property.

*Ordering and the grazing end.* Inverting the increasing angular parametrizations shows that $g_b$ is jointly continuous. Distinct graphs are disjoint, so their order cannot change with $\theta$. For each fixed angle, the continuous injective function $b\mapsto g_b(\theta)$ is strictly monotone, in the same direction at every angle. Reverse $b$ to make this direction decreasing. The upper-strip inclusion <a href="#geo:uniform-collar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:uniform-collar">[geo:uniform-collar]</a> then forces $g_b(\theta)\to h(\theta)$ as $b\downarrow0$ at every angle. Monotonicity and compactness upgrade this to uniform convergence. Formula <a href="#geo:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:reflection">[geo:reflection]</a> now shows that $F_b(\theta)-\theta$ is positive and tends uniformly to zero at that end. Its positive minimum on each fixed graph gives $\tau(b)>0$, and $\tau(b)\to0$ follows.

The forward twist and decreasing graph order give $F_{b_1}<F_{b_2}$ when $b_1<b_2$. Since the maps are increasing, all their positive iterates have the same strict order. Passing to translation numbers shows that $\tau(b)$ is nondecreasing.

*Rational plateaus are impossible.* If $\tau(b)=2\pi l/q$ on an open parameter interval $I$, almost every point in its finite-area invariant annulus is recurrent for $T^q$. On each leaf, $F_b^q-2\pi l$ has zero translation number and hence has fixed points: without a fixed point its periodic displacement would have a uniform strict sign. Every nonfixed point moves monotonically in a component of the complement of the fixed set towards an endpoint, so is not recurrent on the circle. Thus $T^q$ is the identity almost everywhere in this annulus, and therefore everywhere, by continuity and full support of area. Its lift on each graph is necessarily $F_b^q=\mathrm{id}+2\pi l$, with the integer shift determined by the translation number. This contradicts strict ordering of the iterates on two distinct graphs.

The continuity, monotonicity, positivity, and zero limiting value of $\tau$ now show that every sufficiently small positive rational $l/q$ occurs. Its parameter is unique, since two parameters with that value would give a plateau. Values on either side are strictly below and above $2\pi l/q$; taking limits in <a href="#geo:translation-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:translation-sign">[geo:translation-sign]</a> proves <a href="#geo:rational-periodicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:rational-periodicity">[geo:rational-periodicity]</a> on the entire graph. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Closing stationary chains

Proposition <a href="#prop:graph-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph-collar">5</a> supplies the phase conclusion used in (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]. We isolate an immediate variational consequence because it will be used twice: a fixed-endpoint stationary chain closes in momentum as well as in angle. This rational-graph/action connection is related to the periodic-graph regularity studied by Fierobe and Sorrentino (2026, Appendix, Proposition A.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[FierobeSorrentino2026](#ref-FierobeSorrentino2026)]; our argument starts with the full continuous foliation and proves the graph and closure properties before any analytic regularity is known.

<a id="stmt-lem-stationary-closure"></a>
<div id="lem:stationary-closure" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:interior-stationarity"></a>
<a id="geo:chain-momenta"></a>
**Lemma 6**. *There is $\kappa>0$ with the following property. Let $q\ge1$ and $l$ be integers, and let $\theta_0,\ldots,\theta_q$ be a real chain such that $$0<\theta_{j+1}-\theta_j<\kappa,\qquad
 \theta_q=\theta_0+2\pi l,$$ and suppose it is stationary at all interior nodes: $$\label{geo:interior-stationarity}
 \mathcal{S}_2(\theta_{j-1},\theta_j)
             +\mathcal{S}_1(\theta_j,\theta_{j+1})=0,
 \qquad 1\le j<q.$$ Define the momenta, including the final one, by $$\label{geo:chain-momenta}
 p_j=-\mathcal{S}_1(\theta_j,\theta_{j+1})\quad(0\le j<q),
 \qquad p_q=\mathcal{S}_2(\theta_{q-1},\theta_q).$$ Then $\widetilde T(\theta_j,p_j)=(\theta_{j+1},p_{j+1})$ for every $0\le j<q$, and $p_q=p_0$. The chain therefore extends to a periodic orbit of rotation $l/q$, on the unique graph with that rotation. The action $\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}\mathcal{S}(\theta_j,\theta_{j+1})$ is constant along any $C^1$ family of such chains with fixed $q,l$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stationary-closure"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stationary-closure</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $\kappa<2\pi$ small enough that <a href="#geo:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:reflection">[geo:reflection]</a> and <a href="#geo:uniform-collar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:uniform-collar">[geo:uniform-collar]</a> place every line with angular step in $(0,\kappa)$ in $U$. Decrease it further so that Proposition <a href="#prop:graph-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph-collar">5</a> applies to every positive rational smaller than $\kappa/(2\pi)$. The generating relation and <a href="#geo:interior-stationarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:interior-stationarity">[geo:interior-stationarity]</a> give every stated transition; for the last transition use precisely the definition of $p_q$ in <a href="#geo:chain-momenta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:chain-momenta">[geo:chain-momenta]</a>. No endpoint stationarity has been assumed.

The initial state belongs to an individually invariant graph in $U$, so every state of the chain belongs to that same graph. The equality $\theta_q=\theta_0+2\pi l$ therefore forces $p_q=p_0$. Repeating the orbit now shows that its translation number is $2\pi l/q$. Since $0<l/q<\kappa/(2\pi)$, uniqueness of the rational graph applies. In particular, all chains with the same $q,l$ lie on the same graph, regardless of their starting angles.

Finally, differentiating the action along a $C^1$ family cancels all interior terms by <a href="#geo:interior-stationarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:interior-stationarity">[geo:interior-stationarity]</a>. The remaining terms are $-p_0\dot\theta_0+p_q\dot\theta_q=0$, because the endpoint advance is fixed and the momenta agree. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:analytic"></a>
### Analyticity of the boundary

The phase graphs give exact equations for the boundary, one at each sufficiently high Fourier frequency. The decisive analytic fact is that the derivative of these equations is close to the identity on the high modes. We state the two estimates imported from (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 2.3 and Proposition 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)], then prove the regularity conclusion from our phase hypothesis. The grazing coordinate follows the normalization introduced by Lazutkin (1973) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Lazutkin1973](#ref-Lazutkin1973)]; asymptotic grazing actions and rational Fourier tests also appear, in different settings, in Marvizi and Melrose (1982) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[MarviziMelrose1982](#ref-MarviziMelrose1982)], Ramírez-Ros (2006), and Avila, De Simoi, and Kaloshin (2016).

<a id="stmt-thm-analytic-boundary"></a>
<div id="thm:analytic-boundary" class="theorem">

**Theorem 7**. *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the support function $h$ and the boundary of the table are real analytic.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Normalization and the two analytic inputs

<a id="ana:normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:normalized-action"></a>
Choose $k>0$ so that $\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=k r(\theta)^{1/3}$ defines a coordinate of period $2\pi$. With $\theta=\phi(x)$, put $$\label{ana:normalization}
 u=\log\phi',\qquad c_*=k^{-3},\qquad
 r(\phi(x))=c_*e^{-3u(x)}.$$ The normalized action is $$D_u(X,Y)=c_*^{-1}\left\{
 \mathcal{S}(\phi(X),\phi(Y))-
 \int_{\phi(X)}^{\phi(Y)}h(\eta)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\right\}.$$ The endpoint integral telescopes along a chain, so the Euler equations are unchanged by its subtraction and the increasing coordinate change. Writing $\phi(\mu)=(\phi(X)+\phi(Y))/2$, integration by parts gives $$\label{eq:normalized-action}
\begin{split}
D_u(X,Y)={}&-\int_X^\mu
 \bigl(1-\cos(\phi(z)-\phi(X))\bigr)e^{-2u(z)}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\\
 &-\int_\mu^Y
 \bigl(1-\cos(\phi(Y)-\phi(z))\bigr)e^{-2u(z)}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z.
\end{split}$$ Indeed, before the coordinate change, the sum of the two integrals with integrands $r(\eta)(1-\cos(\eta-\theta))$ and $r(\eta)(1-\cos(\theta'-\eta))$ is $\int_\theta^{\theta'}h-\mathcal{S}(\theta,\theta')$; the terms containing $h'$ at the midpoint cancel. Now use $r(\phi(z))\phi'(z)/c_*=e^{-2u(z)}$.

For an arbitrary smooth real periodic function $f$, define $D_f$ by <a href="#eq:normalized-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-action">[eq:normalized-action]</a>, replacing $u$ by $f$ and taking any primitive $\phi_f$ of $e^f$. No condition is imposed on $\int_0^{2\pi}e^f$. Translating both endpoints by $2\pi$ adds the same constant to every primitive value, so $D_f$ is diagonally periodic. The midpoint is well defined for small steps because $\phi_f'>0$ on the real axis. The same formula defines the holomorphic action locally near the diagonal for holomorphic real-symmetric inputs. This arbitrary-input formulation is essential: the trial functions below need not themselves represent billiard tables.

<a id="ana:universal-cubic"></a>
The normalization fixes the leading action independently of the input: $$\label{ana:universal-cubic}
 D_f(x,x+b)=-\frac{b^3}{24}+O(b^5).$$ Indeed, the midpoint is $x+b/2+O(b^2)$, and $\phi_f'^2e^{-2f}=1$, so each half-integral has leading value $b^3/48$. Reversing the endpoints gives $D_f(Y,X)=-D_f(X,Y)$. If $a_3(x),a_4(x)$ are its cubic and quartic coefficients, this identity gives $2a_4=a_3'$; hence the quartic coefficient vanishes. This common cubic term explains the uniform mesh used as the reference in the next proposition. The Fourier-row estimate below separately controls variation with the input.

For $0<\sigma\le1$, let $\mathcal A_\sigma=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\sigma\}$ and set $$\|f\|_{j,\sigma}=
 \max_{0\le i\le j}\sup_{\mathcal A_\sigma}|f^{(i)}|.$$ Inputs on positive strips are periodic, holomorphic, and real symmetric; at $\sigma=0$ they are smooth real periodic functions and the norm is the corresponding real $C^j$ norm.

<a id="stmt-prop-mesh-input"></a>
<div id="prop:mesh-input" class="proposition">

<a id="ana:mesh-bounds"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Stationary meshes, (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]). *Fix an integer $m\ge2$ and a finite bound $M$, and put $p=m+2$, $r_0=m+15$. There are $R,K,N$, depending only on $m,M$, with the following properties. For $0\le\sigma\le1$, $\|f\|_{r_0,\sigma}\le M$, and $q\ge N$, the action $D_f$ has an interior-stationary mesh with endpoints $X_0=x$, $X_q=x+2\pi$, of the form $$X_j(x)=x+2\pi j/q+w_j(x),\qquad w_0=w_q=0.$$ On the strip of width $\sigma'=(1-K/q^2)\sigma$, $$\label{ana:mesh-bounds}
 q^2\max_j\|w_j\|_{p,\sigma'}+
 q^3\max_j\|w_{j+1}-w_j\|_{p,\sigma'}\le R.$$ The mesh is real symmetric and differentiable in smooth real input variations; its derivative in the norm on the left of <a href="#ana:mesh-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:mesh-bounds">[ana:mesh-bounds]</a> is bounded by $C\|\dot f\|_{r_0,\sigma}$. The corresponding difference bound holds for two inputs. On the real axis the mesh is pointwise unique in the ball with these scaled position and step bounds. Any fixed enlargement of that real uniqueness ball is allowed after increasing $N$.*

</div>

Define the critical action and its selected Fourier coefficients by <a id="ana:constraints"></a>$$\label{ana:constraints}
\begin{split}
 W_q(x;f)&=\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}D_f(X_j(x),X_{j+1}(x)),\\
 C_n(f)&=-\frac{q^2}{6\pi}[W_q(\cdot;f)]_n,
 \qquad n=\pm q,\\
 [v]_n&=\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}v(x)e^{-\mathrm{i}nx}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.
\end{split}$$ Thus $W_q$ records how the stationary-chain action depends on its initial point, and $C_q,C_{-q}$ select its $q$-th Fourier harmonics. Periodicity of the displacements and critical action follows from real mesh uniqueness. The same uniqueness identifies these definitions on common real inputs when different strip widths or different fixed smoothness bounds are used, for all sufficiently large rows. In comparing two constructions, one enlarges the real uniqueness ball to contain both meshes. Thus this compatibility is independent of a chosen holomorphic extension.

<a id="stmt-prop-row-input"></a>
<div id="prop:row-input" class="proposition">

<a id="ana:row-bound"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Fourier rows, (OpenAI 2026, Proposition 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]). *For fixed $m\ge2$, $M<\infty$, and $r_0=m+15$, there are $K,N$, independent of $0\le\sigma\le1$, such that for $\|f\|_{r_0,\sigma}\le M$ and $|n|\ge N$, <a id="ana:row-derivative"></a>$$\label{ana:row-derivative}
 (\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}C_n)_f(v)=[b_n(f)v]_n,$$ where <a id="ana:row-difference"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|b_n(f)-1\|_{m,(1-K/n^2)\sigma}&\le K/|n|,
 \label{ana:row-bound}\\
 \|b_n(f)-b_n(\widehat f)\|_{m,(1-K/n^2)\sigma}
 &\le (K/|n|)\|f-\widehat f\|_{r_0,\sigma}.
 \label{ana:row-difference}
\end{aligned}$$ The second estimate holds for two inputs with the same bound. The derivative identity holds for smooth real variations and for real-symmetric holomorphic variations with the indicated finite norms.*

</div>

Both inputs concern the arbitrary-input action <a href="#eq:normalized-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-action">[eq:normalized-action]</a>; neither presupposes physical caustics. We use their estimates directly, without applying the analytic-boundary result of (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)] under its stronger standing geometric hypothesis.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The equations satisfied by the actual boundary

<a id="stmt-ana-vanishing"></a>
<div id="ana:vanishing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *For the smooth function $u$ in <a href="#ana:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:normalization">[ana:normalization]</a>, $C_n(u)=0$ for every sufficiently large $|n|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-vanishing"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-vanishing</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:mesh-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mesh-input">8</a> on the real axis. Its steps are $2\pi/q+O(q^{-3})$, hence positive and uniformly small. Applying $\phi$ gives a stationary chain for $\mathcal{S}$, with positive uniformly small angular increments and total advance $2\pi$. Proposition <a href="#prop:graph-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph-collar">5</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:stationary-closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationary-closure">6</a> show that its final momentum equals its initial momentum. Thus the chain is stationary also at the identified endpoints. More explicitly, differentiation of its critical action cancels every interior term, and the two endpoint terms are $$W_q'(x;u)=\frac{\phi'(x)}{c_*}(p_q-p_0)=0.$$ Here $\phi'(x+2\pi)=\phi'(x)$, and periodicity of $h$ cancels the two endpoint contributions from the subtracted integral. The action is constant in $x$, so its nonzero Fourier coefficients vanish. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A bounded inverse on one Fourier strip

For a Fourier sequence and $a\ge0$, define $$|v|_{\sigma,a}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}
 (1+|k|)^a|[v]_k|e^{\sigma|k|},\qquad
 |v|_\sigma=|v|_{\sigma,0}.$$ The norm $|\cdot|_\sigma$ defines a Wiener space. Let $\Pi_H$ be the projection to $|k|\ge N$, and write $C(f)$ for the sequence of these output rows.

<a id="stmt-ana-wiener"></a>
<div id="ana:wiener" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *Fix the bounds in Proposition <a href="#prop:row-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:row-input">9</a> with $m=2$. The row formula extends to a bounded Fourier operator $$A(f)=\Pi_H+R(f),\qquad
 \|R(f)\|\le C/N,$$ on each Wiener space of width $\sigma$. For two bounded inputs, $$\|R(f)-R(\widehat f)\|
 \le C\|f-\widehat f\|_{r_0,\sigma}.$$ For sufficiently large $N$, the restriction $A_H(f)$ to the high input modes is invertible with $\|A_H(f)^{-1}\|\le2$. The operators and this inverse preserve real Fourier symmetry. With higher fixed input bounds, the same matrix is bounded in $|\cdot|_{\sigma,a}$ whenever $m>a+1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-wiener"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-wiener</summary>

*Proof.* Contour displacement and $m$ integrations by parts in <a href="#ana:row-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:row-bound">[ana:row-bound]</a> give <a id="ana:coefficient-decay"></a>$$\label{ana:coefficient-decay}
 |[b_n(f)-1]_j|\le
 \frac{C}{|n|}(1+|j|)^{-m}
 e^{-(1-K/n^2)\sigma|j|}.$$ For positive $\sigma$, displace first to an interior closed strip and then take its width to the indicated limit; for $\sigma=0$, only integration by parts is needed. Coefficient differences satisfy the same bound multiplied by $\|f-\widehat f\|_{r_0,\sigma}$.

Increase $N$ so that $N^2\ge2K$, and set $\alpha=K/n^2\le1/2$. The triangle inequality yields the useful identity and bound <a id="ana:exponent-balance"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |n|-|k|-(1-\alpha)|n-k|
 &=(1-\alpha)(|n|-|k|-|n-k|)
       +\alpha(|n|-|k|)\notag\\
 &\le \alpha|n|=K/|n|.
 \label{ana:exponent-balance}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently the weighted matrix entry from $k$ to $n$ of $R(f)$ is at most $$\frac{C}{|n|}(1+|n-k|)^{-m}e^{K\sigma/|n|}.$$ At $m=2$ its column sums are $O(N^{-1})$, proving the stated operator bounds on weighted $\ell^1$. A Neumann series gives the inverse. The equality $C_{-q}(f)=\overline{C_q(f)}$ for real inputs gives the real symmetry. Finally, $$\frac{(1+|n|)^a}{(1+|k|)^a}\le(1+|n-k|)^a$$ reduces the polynomially weighted estimate to the convergent sum of $(1+|n-k|)^{a-m}$. ◻

</details>

The Fourier operator here is a bounded extension of the actual derivative on sufficiently regular variations. A Wiener sequence need not have $r_0$ bounded derivatives on its original strip. On every smaller strip it does, and the matrix agrees there with the actual derivative by Fourier-polynomial approximation. Compatibility of the real meshes also identifies the row derivatives across widths. We will only differentiate on segments with the finite input bounds required by Proposition <a href="#prop:row-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:row-input">9</a>.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Analytic approximation and real uniqueness

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-wiener-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-wiener</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:analytic-boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:analytic-boundary">7</a>.* Fix $m=2$, $r_0=17$, and $$M>4e(1+|u|_{0,r_0}).$$ Choose $N$ large enough for Lemmas <a href="#ana:vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:vanishing">10</a> and <a href="#ana:wiener" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:wiener">11</a>, with $\|R\|\le1/2$ throughout this input ball. These choices precede all choices of approximation parameters.

<a id="ana:truncation"></a>
<a id="ana:initial-error"></a>
Let $f_0=\sum_{|k|\le P}[u]_ke^{\mathrm{i}kx}$, where $P>N$, and put $\sigma_0=P^{-1}$. For every fixed $a\ge0$, $$\label{ana:truncation}
 |f_0|_{\sigma_0,a}\le e|u|_{0,a}.$$ We claim that, for every fixed positive integer $a$, $$\label{ana:initial-error}
 |C(f_0)|_{\sigma_0}=O(P^{-a}).$$ For $N\le|n|\le P$, the exponential weights are at most $e$. Integrate the real derivative along the segment from $u$ to $f_0$, use $C(u)=0$, and apply Lemma <a href="#ana:wiener" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:wiener">11</a> at width zero. This part is bounded by $C|f_0-u|_0$, which decays faster than any fixed power of $P$ because $u$ is smooth.

For $|n|>P$, instead integrate along $\lambda f_0$, $0\le\lambda\le1$. The zero input has a uniform stationary mesh and a constant critical action, so $C(0)=0$. For the chosen $a$, use Proposition <a href="#prop:row-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:row-input">9</a> with one fixed integer $m>a+1$.
