Formula <a href="#ana:truncation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:truncation">[ana:truncation]</a> bounds all its required input derivatives uniformly in $P$. Once $P$ exceeds this higher-order row threshold, real mesh uniqueness identifies these rows with the original ones. The final assertion of Lemma <a href="#ana:wiener" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:wiener">11</a> gives $$\sum_{|n|>P}(1+|n|)^a|C_n(f_0)|e^{\sigma_0|n|}
 \le C_a|f_0|_{\sigma_0,a}\le C'_a.$$ Division by $(1+P)^a$ proves the tail bound. This establishes <a href="#ana:initial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:initial-error">[ana:initial-error]</a>; its constants and threshold may depend on $a$, and no uniformity over all smoothness orders is asserted.

<a id="ana:newton-step"></a>
<a id="ana:correction-bound"></a>
<a id="ana:newton-bound"></a>
Keep the modes $|k|<N$ fixed and set $$\sigma_j=\frac{1+2^{-j}}{2P},\qquad
 \sigma_\infty=\frac1{2P},\qquad
 \varepsilon_j=|C(f_j)|_{\sigma_j}.$$ At the $j$-th step define $$\label{ana:newton-step}
 \eta_j=-A_H(f_j)^{-1}C(f_j),\qquad f_{j+1}=f_j+\eta_j.$$ The correction is real symmetric, has only high modes, and obeys $|\eta_j|_{\sigma_j}\le2\varepsilon_j$. Since $\sigma_j-\sigma_{j+1}=2^{-j-2}/P$, the elementary Fourier smoothing estimate gives $$\label{ana:correction-bound}
 \|\eta_j\|_{r_0,\sigma_{j+1}}
 \le C P^{r_0}2^{r_0j}\varepsilon_j.$$ If the correcting segment is in the fixed input ball on this smaller strip, integration of the derivative difference gives $$\label{ana:newton-bound}
 \varepsilon_{j+1}\le
 C\|\eta_j\|_{r_0,\sigma_{j+1}}|\eta_j|_{\sigma_{j+1}}
 \le C P^{r_0}2^{r_0j}\varepsilon_j^2.$$ The linear term cancels by <a href="#ana:newton-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:newton-step">[ana:newton-step]</a>: compatibility of the real row derivatives makes the matrix computed at width $\sigma_j$ the same matrix on the smaller strip. Equivalently, one can integrate each smooth real row first and then take the smaller-strip norm, avoiding any derivative assertion for rough variations at the original strip boundary.

Choose $P$ so large that $\varepsilon_0\le P^{-2r_0}$, possible by <a href="#ana:initial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:initial-error">[ana:initial-error]</a>, and enlarge it further as needed below. The simultaneous induction is <a id="ana:newton-majorant"></a>$$\label{ana:newton-majorant}
 \varepsilon_j\le P^{-2r_0}2^{-(r_0+1)j},
 \qquad
 \|\eta_j\|_{r_0,\sigma_{j+1}}
 \le C P^{-r_0}2^{-j}.$$ Indeed, before using <a href="#ana:newton-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:newton-bound">[ana:newton-bound]</a>, the known bound for $\varepsilon_j$ and <a href="#ana:correction-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:correction-bound">[ana:correction-bound]</a> put the entire current segment in the ball: the initial norm is less than $M/4$, and the cumulative corrections are at most $CP^{-r_0}\sum_{i\ge0}2^{-i}<M/4$. Thus the quadratic estimate is applicable. Its ratio to the proposed bound for $\varepsilon_{j+1}$ is at most $CP^{-r_0}2^{r_0+1-j}$, which is less than one for one sufficiently large fixed $P$. This proves both the error bound and the input bound at every step.

The summable corrections converge on the positive-width strip $\mathcal A_{\sigma_\infty}$, with all derivatives through $r_0$, to a real-symmetric holomorphic function $f_*$. Its low Fourier modes agree with those of $u$, and continuity of each real mesh gives $C_n(f_*)=0$ for every $|n|\ge N$. The real segment between $u$ and $f_*$ lies in the same input ball. Put $v=f_*-u=\Pi_Hv$. Integrating the real derivative, $$0=C(f_*)-C(u)
   =v+\int_0^1R(u+\lambda v)v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda.$$ The bound $\|R\|\le1/2$ implies $|v|_0\le|v|_0/2$, hence $v=0$. The original smooth $u$ is therefore analytic.

Finally $\phi'=e^u>0$, so $\phi$ and its inverse are analytic. Equation <a href="#ana:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:normalization">[ana:normalization]</a> makes $r$ analytic in the normal angle, and the equation $h''+h=r$ makes $h$ analytic. Thus $h n_\theta+h'e_\theta$ is an analytic regular boundary parametrization, proving Theorem <a href="#thm:analytic-boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:analytic-boundary">7</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ana-glancing-interface"></a>
<div id="ana:glancing-interface" class="corollary">

**Corollary 12**. *For the actual table, $D=D_u$ is real symmetric and holomorphic in a uniform neighborhood of the real diagonal. It is diagonally $2\pi$-periodic and antisymmetric, and satisfies <a id="ana:glancing-expansion"></a>$$\label{ana:glancing-expansion}
 D(x,x+b)=-\frac{b^3}{24}+O(b^5),\qquad
 D_1(x,x+b)=\frac{b^2}{8}+O(b^4),$$ locally uniformly with each fixed number of derivatives.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-glancing-interface"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-glancing-interface</summary>

*Proof.* The midpoint implicit function theorem, positivity of $\phi'$ on the real axis, and compactness modulo the period give a uniform holomorphic domain for <a href="#eq:normalized-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-action">[eq:normalized-action]</a>. Reversing its two endpoints gives antisymmetry. The calculation of <a href="#ana:universal-cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:universal-cubic">[ana:universal-cubic]</a> applies on this holomorphic domain. Differentiating at fixed second endpoint is the operator $\partial_x-\partial_b$, which gives the second expansion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:joint"></a>
### Joint analyticity at the grazing boundary

The boundary is now analytic, but the given foliation is still only continuous. We next construct a parametrization that is holomorphic in both its circular variable and its translation parameter. The principal issue is division by difference operators at rational parameters. The periodic graphs from Proposition <a href="#prop:graph-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph-collar">5</a> provide two compatibility conditions: one for the residual and one for its first parameter derivative. Both are needed.

We follow the Newton construction of (OpenAI 2026, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)], giving the arguments that identify its geometric hypotheses in the present setting. The only geometric input in this Section is the invariant graph collar and the stationary-chain closure of Lemma <a href="#lem:stationary-closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationary-closure">6</a>. The Fourier division estimate will be stated separately in its precise form.

<a id="stmt-thm-joint"></a>
<div id="thm:joint" class="theorem">

<a id="joint:stationary"></a>
**Theorem 13** (Joint analytic parametrization). *There are positive numbers $s,d$, with $d<s$, and a function $V(y,t)$, holomorphic on $\{|\Im y|<2s,\ |t|<d\}$, such that $V-y$ is $2\pi$-periodic in $y$, $V$ is even in $t$, and $$V(\bar y,\bar t)=\overline{V(y,t)},\qquad V(y,0)=y.$$ Moreover $V_y$ is uniformly close to one, and $$D_1\bigl(V(y,t),V(y+t,t)\bigr)
 +D_2\bigl(V(y-t,t),V(y,t)\bigr)=0
 \label{joint:stationary}$$ for $|\Im y|<s$, $|t|<d$. In particular $V_y>0$ on the real domain.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The normalized equation

<a id="joint:action"></a>
The analytic action obtained in Section <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">3</a> is antisymmetric and diagonally $2\pi$-periodic. On a fixed complex neighborhood of the real diagonal it satisfies $$D(x,x+b)=-\frac{b^3}{24}+O(b^5),\qquad
 D_1(x,x+b)=\frac{b^2}{8}+b^4B(x,b),
 \label{joint:action}$$ where $B$ is holomorphic and periodic in $x$. All domains below are contained in this fixed action neighborhood.

<a id="joint:a"></a>
Write $T_zf(y,t)=f(y+z,t)$, use a prime for a $y$-derivative, and set $$\partial f=\frac{T_{t/2}f-T_{-t/2}f}{t}.$$ This symbol denotes a difference operator, not a parameter derivative. For a candidate $V$, define <a id="joint:residual"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal R(V)&=D_1(V,T_tV)+D_2(T_{-t}V,V),\notag\\
 E(V)&=t^{-3}V'\mathcal R(V),
 \label{joint:residual}\\
 a(V)&=t^{-1}D_{12}(T_{-t/2}V,T_{t/2}V)
                  (T_{-t/2}V')(T_{t/2}V').
 \label{joint:a}
\end{aligned}$$ For a periodic function, $\langle f\rangle$ denotes its mean in $y$. An assigned first parameter jet prescribes a parameter derivative at one fixed value of $t$, without assuming a nearby family of exact solutions.

<a id="stmt-joint-factorization"></a>
<div id="joint:factorization" class="lemma">

<a id="joint:at-zero"></a>
<a id="joint:linearization"></a>
<a id="joint:parameter-jet"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Regularity and factorization). *The expressions $E$ and $a$ extend holomorphically across $t=0$, with $$E(V)(y,0)=\frac{(V')^2V''}{4},\qquad
 a(V)(y,0)=\frac{(V')^3}{4}.
 \label{joint:at-zero}$$ They are even in $t$ whenever $V$ is even, and $\langle E(V)\rangle=0$. The linearization in a variation $V'w$ is $$E_V(V'w)=\partial(a\partial w)+(Ew)'.
 \label{joint:linearization}$$ At an exact solution at a nonzero real parameter $t_0$, an assigned parameter jet $\dot V=V'z$ gives the total residual jet $$\dot E=\partial\left(a\partial z+\frac{a}{t_0}\right).
 \label{joint:parameter-jet}$$ Here the dot differentiates all parameter dependence, including the explicit shifts.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-joint-factorization"><summary>Proof of stmt-joint-factorization</summary>

<a id="joint:regular-formula"></a>
*Proof.* Antisymmetry gives $\mathcal R=D_1(V,T_tV)-D_1(V,T_{-t}V)$. Put $$A_\pm=\frac{T_{\pm t}V-V}{\pm t}
       =\int_0^1 V'(y\pm ut,t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.$$ By <a href="#joint:action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:action">[joint:action]</a>, $$\frac{E}{V'}=
 \frac{A_++A_-}{8}\frac{A_+-A_-}{t}
 +t\bigl[A_+^4B(V,tA_+)-A_-^4B(V,-tA_-)\bigr].
 \label{joint:regular-formula}$$ The remaining divided difference has the nonsingular expression $$\frac{A_+-A_-}{t}
 =\int_0^1\int_{-u}^{u}V''(y+vt,t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.$$ These formulas prove regularity and the first identity in <a href="#joint:at-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:at-zero">[joint:at-zero]</a>. Since $D_{12}(x,x+b)=b/4+O(b^3)$, the second follows as well. They also show that the first two variations of $E$, on domains with room for the shifts, have bounds in terms of a fixed number of derivatives through order two in $y$. No negative power of $t$ is needed in these bounds. Antisymmetry gives the stated parity.

For real $t$, the mean action $$\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
 D\bigl(V(y+\eta,t),V(y+\eta+t,t)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y$$ is independent of $\eta$. Differentiating at zero and shifting one integral gives $\langle V'\mathcal R\rangle=0$. The mean-zero identity for complex $t$ follows by holomorphic continuation.

For <a href="#joint:linearization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:linearization">[joint:linearization]</a>, separate each off-diagonal variation into its value at $y$ and its difference from that value. With $$c_+=D_{12}(V,T_tV)V'T_tV',\qquad
 c_-=D_{12}(T_{-t}V,V)T_{-t}V'V',$$ the difference terms are $$t^{-3}\bigl[c_+(T_tw-w)+c_-(T_{-t}w-w)\bigr]
 =\partial(a\partial w),$$ since $T_{\pm t/2}a=c_\pm/t$. The common-value terms, including the variation of $V'$, are $(Ew)'$.

At an exact solution, differentiating the factor $t^{-3}$ contributes zero. Differentiation of the explicit shifts contributes $(c_+-c_-)/t_0^3=\partial a/t_0$, whereas the assigned jet contributes $\partial(a\partial z)$. This proves <a href="#joint:parameter-jet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:parameter-jet">[joint:parameter-jet]</a>. ◻

</details>

We will use candidates with $V'$ close to one and $|t|$ small, so <a href="#joint:a" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:a">[joint:a]</a> makes $a$ close to $1/4$. For a Newton correction of the form $\Delta V=V'v$, the main equation is $\partial(a\partial v)\approx-E$: the remaining linearization term $(Ev)'$ is quadratic in the residual and the correction once $v$ is controlled by the residual. We will solve this equation by two approximate primitives, $$\partial H\approx-E,\qquad
 b=a^{-1}(H+\lambda(t)),\quad \langle b\rangle=0,
 \qquad \partial v\approx b.$$ The free constant $\lambda(t)$ makes the second right-hand side have zero mean; each approximation will have error quadratic in the residual norm. At a rational parameter, the Fourier multiplier of $\partial$ vanishes in some nonzero modes. Periodic orbits give compatible residual values for the first division. At those modes, the value of the resulting primitive is determined by a parameter derivative, so compatible first parameter jets are needed to justify the second division. Both compatibilities will hold up to quadratic errors, using jets assigned at individual rational parameters without differentiating the given continuous foliation. We first establish these rational compatibilities and then carry out both divisions with uniform bounds.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Domains and the Newton estimate

For $\nu\in\{0,\tfrac12,1\}$, set <a id="joint:domains"></a>$$\mathcal D^\nu(s,d)=
 \{(y,t):|t|<d,\ |\Im y|<s-\nu|\Im t|\}.
 \label{joint:domains}$$ These three domains track the complex shifts in the two divisions. Starting with $V$ on $\mathcal D^0$, the shifts in the residual require $\mathcal D^1$. The first Fourier division will recover one half of this loss, giving $H$ on $\mathcal D^{1/2}$; the second will give $v$ on $\mathcal D^0$. The radii will decrease slightly at each operation to control derivatives.

Choose $d<\min(1,s/2)$ small. Besides staying in the fixed action domain, we require that every positive real step in the normalized coordinate of size at most $3d$ gives a line in the graph collar, and that all positive rational translations less than $d$ lie in the range of Proposition <a href="#prop:graph-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph-collar">5</a>. Both requirements hold after shrinking $d$: the line momentum tends uniformly to the grazing support as the step tends to zero. We also choose $3d\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\phi'(x)<\kappa$, where $\kappa$ is the angular-step bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:stationary-closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationary-closure">6</a>. Thus every such normalized step has angular increment less than $\kappa$.

Candidates are holomorphic on $\mathcal D^0(s,d)$, real-symmetric, even in $t$, periodic modulo $y$, and satisfy a fixed small bound on $V-y$ and its first three $y$-derivatives. In particular $V'$ is close to one. Equation <a href="#joint:a" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:a">[joint:a]</a> and <a href="#joint:action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:action">[joint:action]</a> then make $a$ uniformly close to $1/4$ on $\mathcal D^{1/2}(s,d)$.

For $0<\delta<d/100$, abbreviate $$\mathcal D_j^\nu=\mathcal D^\nu(s-j\delta,d-j\delta),
 \qquad Y_j=\{y:|\Im y|<s-j\delta\}.$$ Norms without a derivative subscript are supremum norms. Fixed Cauchy losses between these domains cost fixed powers of $\delta^{-1}$. Indeed polydisks of radius $\delta/4$ about points of $\mathcal D_{j+1}^\nu$ lie in $\mathcal D_j^\nu$. A shift by $t/2$ increases the required value of $\nu$ by $1/2$.

<a id="stmt-joint-newton"></a>
<div id="joint:newton" class="proposition">

<a id="joint:step-size"></a>
<a id="joint:step-error"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Uniform Newton step). *There are fixed constants $C_0,B_0$, depending only on the fixed analytic action bounds and candidate smallness bounds, for which the following holds. Suppose $$\|E(V)\|_{\mathcal D_0^1}\le\varepsilon<1,
 \qquad e^{-N\delta}\le\varepsilon,
 \qquad Q=1+N+\delta^{-1},$$ where $N\ge1$ is an integer. If $C_0Q^{B_0}\varepsilon$ is sufficiently small, there is a periodic, real-symmetric, even correction $\Delta V$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \max_{0\le j\le3}\|\partial_y^j\Delta V\|_{\mathcal D_{20}^0}
   &\le C_0Q^{B_0}\varepsilon,
   \label{joint:step-size}\\
 \|E(V+\Delta V)\|_{\mathcal D_{20}^1}
   &\le C_0Q^{B_0}\varepsilon^2.
   \label{joint:step-error}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular $B_0$ is independent of the radius $d$, the retained rational parameters, and the order of a formal initializer.*

</div>

We prove this version of (OpenAI 2026, Proposition 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)] from the graph collar. In its proof, $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^k)$ means a bound $CQ^B\varepsilon^k$, where $C,B$ are fixed. These constants may increase a fixed finite number of times. At the end, a single choice of $C_0,B_0$ includes every bound and every polynomial smallness requirement. None of the following operations increases the number of derivatives with $N$ or with the initializer order.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Rational values and first parameter jets

<a id="stmt-joint-compatibility"></a>
<div id="joint:compatibility" class="lemma">

<a id="joint:value-compatibility"></a>
<a id="joint:jet-compatibility"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Rational compatibility). *Let $t_0=2\pi l/q\ne0$ be reduced, with $q\le N$ and $|t_0|<d-2\delta$. Under the hypotheses and polynomial smallness conditions of Proposition <a href="#joint:newton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:newton">15</a>, there are holomorphic periodic jets $w,\dot w$ on $Y_{10}$, real-symmetric in $y$, satisfying $$\begin{aligned}
 w,\dot w&=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon),\notag\\
 E+\partial(a\partial w)&=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2),
 \label{joint:value-compatibility}\\
 \bigl(E+\partial(a\partial w)\bigr)^{\displaystyle\cdot}
   &=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2).
 \label{joint:jet-compatibility}
\end{aligned}$$ The dot in the last line uses the assigned jet $\dot w$ and differentiates every occurrence of $t$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-joint-compatibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-joint-compatibility</summary>

*Proof.* We begin with $t_0>0$. Write $U_j(y)=V(y+jt_0,t_0)$ and seek interior-stationary nodes $$X_j=U_j+U'_j\xi_j,\qquad 0\le j\le q,
 \qquad \xi_0=\xi_q=0.$$ The endpoint increment is $2\pi l$. Multiply the interior stationarity equation at node $j$ by the fixed factor $t_0^{-3}U'_j$. At $\xi=0$ the residual is $E(y+jt_0,t_0)$. By <a href="#joint:linearization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:linearization">[joint:linearization]</a>, its derivative in $\xi$ is the Dirichlet flux matrix <a id="joint:dirichlet"></a>$$(A_0\xi)_j=t_0^{-2}
 \bigl[a_{j+1/2}(\xi_{j+1}-\xi_j)
       -a_{j-1/2}(\xi_j-\xi_{j-1})\bigr],
 \quad a_{j+1/2}=a(y+(j+1/2)t_0,t_0),
 \label{joint:dirichlet}$$ plus the diagonal potential $$W_j=T_{jt_0}\left(E'-\frac{V''}{V'}E\right).$$ The prefactor in the finite-dimensional equations is frozen; subtracting its variation explains the second term in this potential.

The inverse of $A_0$ has a uniform complex bound. For $A_0\xi=f$, let $J_{j+1/2}=a_{j+1/2}(\xi_{j+1}-\xi_j)$. Then $$J_{j+1/2}=J_{1/2}+t_0^2\sum_{i=1}^j f_i.$$ The condition $\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}(\xi_{j+1}-\xi_j)=0$ determines $J_{1/2}$. Since each $a_{j+1/2}^{-1}$ is close to four, the modulus of their sum is at least a fixed positive multiple of $q$. Summing the resulting increments gives <a id="joint:dirichlet-inverse"></a>$$\|A_0^{-1}\|_{\infty\to\infty}\le Cq^2t_0^2\le CQ^2.
 \label{joint:dirichlet-inverse}$$ On $Y_1$, the potential is bounded by $CQ\varepsilon$. Thus a Neumann series gives the same bound for the full inverse if $CQ^3\varepsilon$ is small.

<a id="joint:rational-bound"></a>
Reducedness supplies the useful lower bound $$|t_0|^{-1}\le q/(2\pi)\le N/(2\pi).
 \label{joint:rational-bound}$$ The nonlinear equations involve only three neighboring nodes, bounded fixed derivatives of $D$, and the factor $t_0^{-3}$. Their Hessian in the supremum norm therefore has size at most $CQ^3$, independently of the number of nodes. Applying the linearized inverse to the nonlinear remainder defines a contraction on a ball of radius $CQ^2\varepsilon$ when $CQ^7\varepsilon$ is small. Hence $\xi=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ on $Y_1$, holomorphically in $y$. Uniqueness preserves periodicity and real symmetry. Fixed derivatives are controlled on smaller strips by Cauchy’s estimate.

For real $y$, the uncorrected increments are bounded below by a fixed multiple of $t_0$. The correction, using <a href="#joint:rational-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:rational-bound">[joint:rational-bound]</a> and a stronger fixed polynomial smallness condition, preserves positivity and keeps every step below $3d$. After applying $\phi$, these are actual stationary billiard chains: subtracting the endpoint primitive in the definition of $D$ telescopes, and the coordinate change has nonzero derivative. Their initial states lie in the graph collar. Lemma <a href="#lem:stationary-closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationary-closure">6</a> therefore gives momentum return as well as angle return. Each chain is periodic with rotation $l/q$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:graph-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph-collar">5</a> identifies the same unique invariant graph for every starting $y$.

Invert $V(\,\cdot\,,t_0)$ locally around the uncorrected nodes to write $$X_j(y)=V(\widetilde y_j(y),t_0),\qquad
 \widetilde y_j=y+jt_0+O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$$ on $Y_2$. This inverse is obtained by contraction for the small displacement from $y+jt_0$; it requires no assertion about a global complex inverse. On the real axis, uniqueness of the rational graph implies $\widetilde y_j=\widetilde y_1^{\,j}$ and $\widetilde y_q=y+2\pi l$. Consequently $$G(y)=\frac1q\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}\bigl(\widetilde y_j(y)-jt_0\bigr)
 \quad\hbox{satisfies}\quad
 G(\widetilde y_1(y))=G(y)+t_0.$$ Its displacement and, on $Y_3$, its derivative displacement are $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$. Contraction gives an inverse on $Y_4$. Thus <a id="joint:rational-solution"></a>$$V_*(y)=V(G^{-1}(y),t_0)
 \label{joint:rational-solution}$$ is an exact solution at $t_0$, with $V_*-V(\,\cdot\,,t_0)=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$. The stationarity identity follows first on the real axis from the graph dynamics and then throughout the strip by holomorphy.

<a id="joint:raw-jet"></a>
We must also give $V_*$ an appropriate first parameter jet. Initially assign $\dot V_*=V_t(\,\cdot\,,t_0)$. Its residual jet is $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ on $Y_6$. Indeed Cauchy’s estimate gives $E_t=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ there: the distance of $t_0$ from the parameter boundary exceeds $2\delta$. A polydisk of radius $\delta/4$ in each variable about $(y,t_0)$, with $y\in Y_6$, lies in $\mathcal D_0^1$, so this estimate costs only $C\delta^{-1}$. Replacing $V$ by $V_*$, with the same assigned jet, changes that derivative by $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$. To make its polynomial character explicit, if $J$ is any assigned jet, direct differentiation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \dot E={}&-3t^{-4}V'\mathcal R+t^{-3}J'\mathcal R\notag\\
 &+t^{-3}V'\bigl[
 D_{11}(V,T_tV)J+D_{12}(V,T_tV)(T_tJ+T_tV')\notag\\
 &\hspace{29mm}
 +D_{21}(T_{-t}V,V)(T_{-t}J-T_{-t}V')
 +D_{22}(T_{-t}V,V)J\bigr].
 \label{joint:raw-jet}
\end{aligned}$$ Only fixed derivatives occur, $V_t$ and its needed $y$-derivatives have Cauchy bounds, and <a href="#joint:rational-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:rational-bound">[joint:rational-bound]</a> controls every denominator.

Let $\Pi=q^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}T_{jt_0}$. This is a projection on periodic functions, and $\Pi\partial=\partial\Pi=0$. Equation <a href="#joint:parameter-jet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:parameter-jet">[joint:parameter-jet]</a> at the exact solution shows that $\Pi\dot E_*=0$. Put $a_*=a(V_*)$; after the fixed derivative losses, it is still close to $1/4$. On $\ker\Pi$, the operator $\partial(a_*\partial)$ has an inverse with polynomial norm. For an explicit verification, put $T=T_{t_0}$ and $$R_q=\frac1q\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}jT^j.$$ The identity $(T-1)R_q=1-\Pi$ gives $\partial^{-1}=t_0R_qT_{t_0/2}$ on $\ker\Pi$, of norm at most $Cq|t_0|$. Projected multiplication $(1-\Pi)a_*:\ker\Pi\to\ker\Pi$ differs from multiplication by $1/4$ by at most $2\|a_*-1/4\|$, so is invertible by a Neumann series. Composing these three inverses yields $$\|\{\partial(a_*\partial)\}^{-1}\|_{\ker\Pi}
 \le Cq^2t_0^2.$$ The projection commutes with all translations; no trivial action of the half-shift on its range is needed. We may therefore correct $\dot V_*$ by $V_*'z$, where $z=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$, to make its total residual jet zero.

Finally set $w=(V_*-V)/V'$ and differentiate this quotient using the corrected jet of $V_*$. Both $w$ and $\dot w$, with the fixed number of derivatives needed below, are $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ after the losses up to $Y_{10}$. Taylor’s formula and <a href="#joint:linearization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:linearization">[joint:linearization]</a> give $$0=E(V_*)=E+\partial(a\partial w)+(Ew)'
              +O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2).$$ The same formula differentiated in the assigned jets has a quadratic remainder. To see this directly, write the remainder as the integral of the second variation along the segment from $V$ to $V_*$. A first parameter derivative either replaces one of its two increments by its assigned jet, or differentiates a coefficient. The first case still contains two $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ factors. The second costs only fixed derivatives in <a href="#joint:raw-jet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:raw-jet">[joint:raw-jet]</a>, with denominators at worst $t_0^{-4}$, and also retains both factors. Cauchy estimates on the remaining fixed buffers absorb the required derivatives. Moreover $(Ew)'$ and its jet are quadratic since $E,E_t,w,\dot w=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$. This proves both <a href="#joint:value-compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:value-compatibility">[joint:value-compatibility]</a> and <a href="#joint:jet-compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:jet-compatibility">[joint:jet-compatibility]</a>. All costs are fixed powers of $Q$: the mesh inverse costs $Q^2$, its Hessian $Q^3$, the cyclic inverse at most $Q^2$, and all other steps use fixed derivatives and fixed Cauchy losses. Negative $t_0$ follow by evenness, with the sign of the first jet reversed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Two Fourier divisions

For a periodic function $F$, write $F(y,t)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}F_n(t)e^{\mathrm{i}ny}$. The nonzero-mode divisor of $\partial$ is <a id="joint:divisor"></a>$$d_n(t)=\frac{2\mathrm{i}\sin(nt/2)}{t},\qquad d_n(0)=\mathrm{i}n.
 \label{joint:divisor}$$ Its nonzero zeros $t_j=2\pi j/n$, $j\ne0$, are simple. We use the following analytic estimate, with exactly the buffers needed here.

<a id="stmt-joint-division"></a>
<div id="joint:division" class="lemma">

<a id="joint:interpolation"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Buffered division, (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]). *Let $\nu\in\{1/2,1\}$, and suppose a mean-zero periodic function $F$ is holomorphic on $\mathcal D_{j_0}^\nu$, with norm at most $M$. Let the fixed integer buffers satisfy $b_{\mathrm{buf}}\ge j_0+1$ and $a_{\mathrm{buf}}\ge j_0$. Suppose, for $1\le |n|\le N$ and every divisor zero with $0<|t_j|<d-b_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta$, that $$|F_n(t_j)|\le\eta e^{-|n|(s-a_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta)}.$$ For the retained modes define <a id="joint:quotient"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 R_n(t)&=\sum_{0<|t_j|<d-b_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta} F_n(t_j)
       \frac{\sin(n(t-t_j)/2)}{n(t-t_j)/2},
       \label{joint:interpolation}\\
 H_n(t)&=\frac{F_n(t)-R_n(t)}{d_n(t)},
       \label{joint:quotient}
\end{aligned}$$ and set $H_0=0$ and $H_n=0$ for $|n|>N$. The quotients are holomorphic for $|t|<d-(b_{\mathrm{buf}}+1)\delta$. For each fixed integer $J>\max(a_{\mathrm{buf}},j_0,b_{\mathrm{buf}}+1)$, <a id="joint:division-bounds"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|H\|_{\mathcal D_J^{\nu-1/2}}&\le CQ^B(M+\eta),\notag\\
 \|\partial H-F\|_{\mathcal D_J^\nu}
   &\le CQ^B\bigl(\eta+Me^{-N\delta}\bigr).
 \label{joint:division-bounds}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $C,B$ depend on the fixed buffers, not on $N,d,\delta$. Fixed additional derivatives cost fixed additional powers after fixed domain losses. Real symmetry and evenness are preserved.*

</div>

<a id="joint:interpolation-jet"></a>
The interpolation in <a href="#joint:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:interpolation">[joint:interpolation]</a> cancels precisely the included zeros, since its sinc kernel is one at its own center and zero at every other center. The buffers ensure that every possible pole in the smaller parameter disk has been canceled. The gain of one half in the domain exponent comes from the exponential factor $e^{-|n\Im t|/2}$ in the inverse sine divisor. The estimate also provides, on the real parameter axis, $$|R_n(t)|+N^{-1}|R_n'(t)|
   \le CQ^B\eta e^{-|n|(s-a_{\mathrm{buf}}\delta)}.
 \label{joint:interpolation-jet}$$ This follows directly from the sinc formula: there are at most $CN$ centers and the kernel and its first real derivative are bounded by $C$ and $C|n|$. Thus the cited estimate includes all derivative information used below.

<details id="proof-stmt-joint-division"><summary>Proof of stmt-joint-division</summary>

<a id="joint:first-division"></a>
<a id="joint:mean-correction"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#joint:newton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:newton">15</a>.* The first division takes $F=-E$. Its mean vanishes by Lemma <a href="#joint:factorization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:factorization">14</a>. Every nonzero divisor zero for a mode $|n|\le N$ has reduced denominator at most $N$. At those centers with $|t_j|<d-3\delta$, Equation <a href="#joint:value-compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:value-compatibility">[joint:value-compatibility]</a> and $d_n(t_j)=0$ give $$|E_n(t_j)|\le CQ^B\varepsilon^2e^{-|n|(s-10\delta)}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#joint:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:division">17</a> with $$(j_0,\nu,a_{\mathrm{buf}},b_{\mathrm{buf}},J)=(0,1,10,3,11).$$ Denote the result by $H$. Then $$H=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)\quad\hbox{on }\mathcal D_{11}^{1/2},
 \qquad
 \partial H+E=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)
          \quad\hbox{on }\mathcal D_{11}^{1}.
 \label{joint:first-division}$$ The extra polynomial factor multiplying $\varepsilon^2$ in the bound for $H$ is absorbed by the smallness requirement. Define $$b=a^{-1}(H+\lambda),\qquad
 \lambda(t)=-\frac{\langle a^{-1}H\rangle}{\langle a^{-1}\rangle}.
 \label{joint:mean-correction}$$ The denominator stays away from zero, so $b$ has mean zero and is $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ on $\mathcal D_{12}^{1/2}$.

<a id="joint:comparison"></a>
Before dividing again, we need the compatibility of $b$ at its resonances. Fix a retained rational parameter $t_0$ with $|t_0|<d-14\delta$, and let $g=a\partial w$ use the value and assigned jet from Lemma <a href="#joint:compatibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:compatibility">16</a>. For every retained nonzero mode, the exact first-division identity gives, as an identity of values and first jets at $t_0$, $$d_n(H_n-g_n)=-(E+\partial g)_n-R_n.
 \label{joint:comparison}$$ The right-hand side and its first jet are bounded by $CQ^B\varepsilon^2e^{-|n|(s-10\delta)}$, using both compatibility equations and <a href="#joint:interpolation-jet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:interpolation-jet">[joint:interpolation-jet]</a>.

If $d_n(t_0)\ne0$, reducedness of $t_0/(2\pi)=l/q$ gives $$|\sin(\pi nl/q)|\ge c/q,\qquad
 |d_n(t_0)|^{-1}\le Cq|t_0|.$$ If $d_n(t_0)=0$, then $|d_n'(t_0)|=|n|/|t_0|$, and differentiating <a href="#joint:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:comparison">[joint:comparison]</a> bounds the same difference $H_n-g_n$. Thus in both cases $$|H_n(t_0)-g_n(t_0)|
 \le CQ^B\varepsilon^2 e^{-|n|(s-10\delta)}.$$ This is the reason for the first-jet compatibility: the value equation alone would give no estimate for a resonant coefficient of $g$.

<a id="joint:second-compatibility"></a>
The function $g$ has norm $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$ on $Y_{10}$. Its omitted Fourier tail on $Y_{13}$ is bounded by $CQ^B\varepsilon e^{-3N\delta}=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)$. Summing the retained estimates on that smaller strip therefore gives $$H-(g-g_0)=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)\quad\hbox{on }Y_{13}.$$ Since $\langle a^{-1}g\rangle=\langle\partial w\rangle=0$, the mean correction in <a href="#joint:mean-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:mean-correction">[joint:mean-correction]</a> satisfies $\lambda=g_0+O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)$. Hence $$b-\partial w=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)\quad\hbox{on }Y_{13}.
 \label{joint:second-compatibility}$$ At a zero of $d_n$, the coefficient $(\partial w)_n$ vanishes. Equation <a href="#joint:second-compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:second-compatibility">[joint:second-compatibility]</a> supplies the second division with its required Fourier values.

<a id="joint:second-division"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#joint:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:division">17</a> to $F=b$, with $$(j_0,\nu,a_{\mathrm{buf}},b_{\mathrm{buf}},J)=(12,1/2,13,15,17).$$ All its centers satisfy the comparison range $|t_0|<d-14\delta$. Its output $v$ satisfies $$v=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)\quad\hbox{on }\mathcal D_{17}^{0},
 \qquad
 \partial v-b=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)
       \quad\hbox{on }\mathcal D_{17}^{1/2}.
 \label{joint:second-division}$$ Both divisions, multiplication by $a^{-1}$, and the mean adjustment preserve parity and real symmetry.

Since $ab=H+\lambda(t)$ and $\partial\lambda=0$, <a href="#joint:first-division" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:first-division">[joint:first-division]</a> and <a href="#joint:second-division" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:second-division">[joint:second-division]</a> yield $$E+\partial(a\partial v)=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)
       \quad\hbox{on }\mathcal D_{18}^{1}.$$ Here the remaining difference costs only a derivative and a domain loss, by $\partial f=\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}f'(y+ut,t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u$. Take $\Delta V=V'v$. Cauchy estimates between indices 17 and 20 bound its first three $y$-derivatives by $O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon)$. Equation <a href="#joint:linearization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:linearization">[joint:linearization]</a> makes its linearized residual quadratic, because $(Ev)'=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)$. The nonsingular Formula <a href="#joint:regular-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:regular-formula">[joint:regular-formula]</a> gives $$E(V+\Delta V)-E(V)-E_V(\Delta V)=O_{\mathrm p}(\varepsilon^2)
       \quad\hbox{on }\mathcal D_{20}^{1},$$ including $t=0$.

The construction uses fixed numbers of matrix inversions, contractions, products and derivatives, two applications of Lemma <a href="#joint:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:division">17</a>, and fixed Cauchy losses. All dimension and resonance costs are bounded by powers of $N$, and all shrinking-domain costs by powers of $\delta^{-1}$. The action bounds are fixed before $d$ is shrunk. A single exponent $B_0$ and constant $C_0$ thus give <a href="#joint:step-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:step-size">[joint:step-size]</a>–<a href="#joint:step-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:step-error">[joint:step-error]</a> and absorb every smallness condition. This proves the Proposition with the asserted uniformity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Initialization and convergence

<a id="joint:initializer"></a>
For any fixed integer $m\ge1$, there are analytic periodic coefficients $v_1,\ldots,v_m$ such that $$V^{(m)}(y,t)=y+\sum_{j=1}^m v_j(y)t^{2j},\qquad
 E(V^{(m)})=O(t^{2m+2}).
 \label{joint:initializer}$$ Here is the recursion, also given in (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 3.6) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]. At order $t^{2j}$, the new coefficient enters as $v_j''/4$, by <a href="#joint:at-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:at-zero">[joint:at-zero]</a>; all other terms depend on earlier coefficients. Their mean is zero by the mean-zero identity for $E$. Solve the periodic second-derivative equation, choosing $v_j$ of mean zero. Each fixed finite recursion uses only finitely many derivatives of functions holomorphic on the original open strip. Thus every such recursion is valid on one fixed smaller strip, with constants allowed to depend on $m$. Integration preserves holomorphy and real symmetry. In particular, on that fixed strip and for small $d_0$, $$\|E(V^{(m)})\|\le C_m d_0^{2m+2},\qquad
 \|V^{(m)}-y\|_{C_y^3}\le C_m d_0^2.$$ No bound uniform in $m$ is required.

<details id="proof-stmt-joint-division-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-joint-division</summary>

<a id="joint:error-iteration"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint">13</a>.* Fix a sufficiently small $y$-width $s_0$; all constants in Proposition <a href="#joint:newton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:newton">15</a> are now fixed. Begin with <a href="#joint:initializer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:initializer">[joint:initializer]</a> on radius $d_0<s_0/4$, choosing $m$ and then $d_0$ below. At step $i$, take $$\delta_i=2^{-i}d_0/200$$ and reduce both current radii by $20\delta_i$. Their limits satisfy $d_\infty\ge4d_0/5$ and $s_\infty\ge s_0-d_0/5$. If the current residual norm is $\varepsilon_i>0$, choose $$N_i=\left\lceil\delta_i^{-1}\log(1/\varepsilon_i)\right\rceil.$$ If it vanishes, an exact solution has already been obtained. Every fixed power of $1+\log(1/\varepsilon_i)$ is bounded by a constant times $\varepsilon_i^{-1/4}$. Thus, with one fixed enlarged exponent $B$, Proposition <a href="#joint:newton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="joint:newton">15</a> gives <a id="joint:correction-iteration"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \varepsilon_{i+1}&\le C d_0^{-B}2^{Bi}\varepsilon_i^{7/4},
 \label{joint:error-iteration}\\
 \|\Delta V_i\|_{C_y^3}
   &\le C d_0^{-B}2^{Bi}\varepsilon_i^{3/4}
 \label{joint:correction-iteration}
\end{aligned}$$ on the next domains, provided the polynomial smallness requirement holds.

Choose $m$ so that $2m+2>4B+4$, and only then shrink $d_0$. The finite constant $C_m$ is thereby absorbed, giving the initial bound in the induction <a id="joint:majorant"></a>$$\varepsilon_i\le d_0^{4B+4}2^{-(4B+4)i}.
 \label{joint:majorant}$$ Indeed substituting this bound in <a href="#joint:error-iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:error-iteration">[joint:error-iteration]</a> and dividing by the desired next bound gives at most $$C d_0^{2B+3}2^{4B+4-(2B+3)i},$$ which is at most one for small $d_0$. The correction bound becomes $$\|\Delta V_i\|_{C_y^3}
 \le C d_0^{2B+3}2^{-(2B+3)i}.$$ These bounds both imply the polynomial smallness condition at every step and keep the candidates in their fixed smallness class: the initializer has slack and the total correction is arbitrarily small as $d_0\to0$. The induction is therefore justified before each application of the Newton estimate. The order of choices is $B$, then $m$, then $d_0$.

The corrections converge uniformly, with their first three $y$-derivatives, on the limiting domains. Their limit is holomorphic, real-symmetric, even in $t$, and periodic modulo $y$, with $V_y$ close to one. The residual tends to zero, so <a href="#joint:stationary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:stationary">[joint:stationary]</a> holds wherever the two shifts remain in the limit domain. At $t=0$, Equation <a href="#joint:at-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="joint:at-zero">[joint:at-zero]</a> gives $V''=0$; periodicity gives $V(y,0)=y+c$, with real $c$. Replacing $y$ by $y-c$ removes the constant and preserves the equation and all symmetries. Finally choose a smaller product neighborhood with enough $y$-margin for both shifts. This gives precisely the domain and conclusions stated in the Theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:caustics"></a>
### A physical collar of analytic caustics

We now pass from the joint solution of the stationarity equation to curves in the table. The essential point is that their support functions move strictly inward to first order in the parameter $\lambda=t^2$. For phase-to-caustic correspondences under smoother initial foliations, compare Glutsyuk (2024, sec. 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Glutsyuk2024](#ref-Glutsyuk2024)]; the continuous phase leaves here require the preceding graph and analytic regularization arguments.

<a id="stmt-prop-physical-collar"></a>
<div id="prop:physical-collar" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18**. *There exist $\lambda_0>0$ and a jointly real-analytic family $$\Gamma_\lambda:\mathbb{T}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^2,
 \qquad 0\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_0,$$ with the following properties. The zero leaf parametrizes $\partial\Omega$; each positive leaf is a smooth, simple, regular, strictly convex caustic contained in $\Omega$; and the map $$(\theta,b)\longmapsto\Gamma_{b\lambda_0}(\theta),
 \qquad (\theta,b)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,1),$$ is a homeomorphism onto a relatively open neighborhood of $\partial\Omega$ in $\overline\Omega$. Joint real analyticity here means extension to a neighborhood of the closed parameter cylinder.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-physical-collar"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-physical-collar</summary>

<a id="phys:line-family"></a>
<a id="phys:stationarity"></a>
<a id="phys:invariance"></a>
*Proof.* Let $V$ be the function supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint">13</a>, and set $$\label{phys:line-family}
 \Theta(y,t)=\phi(V(y,t)),\qquad
 p(y,t)=-\mathcal{S}_1\bigl(\Theta(y,t),\Theta(y+t,t)\bigr).$$ For real $y,t$ sufficiently close to the zero-parameter circle, $\Theta_y>0$ and $$\Theta(y+2\pi,t)=\Theta(y,t)+2\pi.$$ The normalization in <a href="#eq:normalized-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-action">[eq:normalized-action]</a> changes a chain action only by a nonzero constant factor and a telescoping endpoint term. Consequently, the equation in Theorem <a href="#thm:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint">13</a> gives $$\label{phys:stationarity}
 \mathcal{S}_1\bigl(\Theta(y,t),\Theta(y+t,t)\bigr)
 +\mathcal{S}_2\bigl(\Theta(y-t,t),\Theta(y,t)\bigr)=0.$$ For sufficiently small $t>0$, successive angles have positive small increments. The generating relation for the actual billiard map and <a href="#phys:stationarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:stationarity">[phys:stationarity]</a>, shifted once in $y$, therefore give $$\label{phys:invariance}
 T\bigl(\Theta(y,t),p(y,t)\bigr)
   =\bigl(\Theta(y+t,t),p(y+t,t)\bigr).$$ In particular, these line states form an invariant graph, and the invariance is onto: the parameter map is the circle translation $y\mapsto y+t$.

<a id="phys:inward-expansion"></a>
Both functions in <a href="#phys:line-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:line-family">[phys:line-family]</a> are even in $t$. For $\Theta$ this follows from the parity of $V$. For $p$, use antisymmetry of $\mathcal{S}$ and <a href="#phys:stationarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:stationarity">[phys:stationarity]</a>: $$\begin{aligned}
 p(y,-t)
 &=-\mathcal{S}_1\bigl(\Theta(y,t),\Theta(y-t,t)\bigr)\\
 &=\mathcal{S}_2\bigl(\Theta(y-t,t),\Theta(y,t)\bigr)
 =p(y,t).
\end{aligned}$$ At $t=0$, the generating relation gives $p(y,0)=h(\Theta(y,0))$. There is also a quantitative inward displacement. Put $$a(y,t)=\frac{\Theta(y+t,t)-\Theta(y,t)}2.$$ The line-coordinate formula of Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">2</a>, expanded at fixed $\theta=\Theta(y,t)$, reads $$p=h(\theta+a)\cos a-h'(\theta+a)\sin a
   =h(\theta)-\frac12r(\theta)a^2+O(a^3).$$ Since $a(y,t)=\frac12\Theta_y(y,0)t+O(t^2)$, and the displacement $h(\Theta)-p$ is even and analytic in $t$, this implies $$\label{phys:inward-expansion}
 h(\Theta(y,t))-p(y,t)
 =\frac18r(\Theta(y,0))\Theta_y(y,0)^2t^2+O(t^4).$$ The expansion and its derivatives are uniform on the real circle.

<a id="phys:fixed-angle-expansion"></a>
<a id="phys:support-inequalities"></a>
An even holomorphic function of $t$ is holomorphic in $\lambda=t^2$ near zero, as is seen from its convergent power series. Write the resulting functions as $\widehat\Theta(y,\lambda)$ and $\widehat p(y,\lambda)$. The analytic inverse function theorem, positivity of $\widehat\Theta_y$, and degree one give a jointly real-analytic angular inverse $y=Y(\theta,\lambda)$, with its natural $2\pi$-equivariance. Compactness of the circle makes the parameter neighborhood uniform. Define the periodic function $$g(\theta,\lambda)=\widehat p(Y(\theta,\lambda),\lambda).$$ It is the support candidate for the graph in <a href="#phys:invariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:invariance">[phys:invariance]</a>. In particular, $g(\theta,0)=h(\theta)$. Substituting the angular inverse in <a href="#phys:inward-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:inward-expansion">[phys:inward-expansion]</a> gives, now at fixed $\theta$, $$\label{phys:fixed-angle-expansion}
 g(\theta,\lambda)=h(\theta)-c(\theta)\lambda+O(\lambda^2),
 \qquad
 c(\theta)=\frac18r(\theta)
       \Theta_y(Y(\theta,0),0)^2>0.$$ Thus no change of angle can remove the strict inward derivative. Choose $\lambda_0>0$ strictly inside the common analytic parameter neighborhood. Decreasing it if necessary, we may arrange on the entire closed cylinder $0\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_0$ that $$\label{phys:support-inequalities}
 g>0,\qquad
 \rho:=g+g_{\theta\theta}>0,\qquad
 g_\lambda\leq-c_0<0,$$ where $c_0$ is a constant. The first two inequalities follow from $h>0$ and $r=h+h''>0$, and the third from <a href="#phys:fixed-angle-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:fixed-angle-expansion">[phys:fixed-angle-expansion]</a>.

For each $\lambda$ define <a id="phys:body-and-boundary"></a>$$\label{phys:body-and-boundary}
 \begin{split}
 K_\lambda
   &=\bigcap_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}
       \{z\in\mathbb{R}^2:z\cdot n_\theta\leq g(\theta,\lambda)\},\\
 \Gamma_\lambda(\theta)
   &=g(\theta,\lambda)n_\theta
     +g_\theta(\theta,\lambda)e_\theta.
 \end{split}$$ Figure <a href="#fig:phase-to-caustic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:phase-to-caustic">1</a> illustrates this construction: a point of the line graph specifies a support line, and the formula for $\Gamma_\lambda$ gives its contact point. We next verify that these formal envelopes are the boundaries of the bodies $K_\lambda$.

<figure id="fig:phase-to-caustic">

<figcaption>The support-envelope correspondence, drawn schematically. The highlighted point of $p = g(θ,λ)$ specifies the blue tangent line on the right; its contact point is $Γ_{λ}(θ_{0}) = gn_{θ0} + g_{θ}e_{θ0}$, with $g$ and $g_{θ}$ evaluated at $(θ_{0},λ)$. Positive $g + g_{θθ}$ makes each envelope strictly convex, and $g_{λ} < 0$ makes the family strictly nested.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-phase-to-caustic"></a>

Visual witness description of PDF page 25 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two schematic diagrams side by side illustrating the support-envelope correspondence:
- Left diagram ("analytic invariant line graphs"): A Cartesian coordinate system with horizontal axis labeled $\theta$ and vertical axis labeled $p$. Two oscillating curves are plotted: an upper curve labeled $h$ and a lower purple curve labeled $g(\cdot, \lambda)$. A point on $g(\cdot, \lambda)$ at coordinate $\theta_0$ is highlighted with a blue dot, with dashed projection lines extending down to $\theta_0$ on the $\theta$-axis and left to the $p$-axis.
- Right diagram ("nested support envelopes"): Two nested convex planar curves. The outer closed curve is labeled $\partial\Omega$. The inner closed curve is indicated by an arrow as "support envelope". A blue tangent line touches the support envelope at a highlighted point labeled $\Gamma_\lambda(\theta_0)$. The line is labeled $z \cdot n_{\theta_0} = g(\theta_0, \lambda)$. A normal vector arrow perpendicular to the tangent line at the contact point is labeled $n_{\theta_0}$.
```


Each $K_\lambda$ is a compact convex body with nonempty interior: it contains a disk about the origin of radius $\min_\theta g(\theta,\lambda)>0$, and the constraint with normal $z/|z|$ bounds $|z|$ by $\max_\theta g(\theta,\lambda)$. Moreover $K_0=\overline\Omega$, by the definition of $h$.

<a id="phys:normal-parametrization"></a>
We verify directly that $\Gamma_\lambda$ is the whole boundary and has support function $g(\cdot,\lambda)$. Differentiation gives $$\label{phys:normal-parametrization}
 \Gamma_\lambda'(\theta)=\rho(\theta,\lambda)e_\theta.$$ For a fixed angle $\alpha$, $$\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}
   \bigl(\Gamma_\lambda(\theta)\cdot n_\alpha\bigr)
 =\rho(\theta,\lambda)\sin(\alpha-\theta).$$ This projection has its unique maximum, modulo $2\pi$, at $\theta=\alpha$, where its value is $g(\alpha,\lambda)$. Hence every point $\Gamma_\lambda(\theta)$ satisfies all the constraints defining $K_\lambda$, with equality for its own normal, and belongs to $\partial K_\lambda$.

Conversely, let $z\in\partial K_\lambda$. The continuous nonnegative function $g(\theta,\lambda)-z\cdot n_\theta$ must vanish somewhere: a positive minimum would place a disk about $z$ inside $K_\lambda$. At a zero $\alpha$, its derivative also vanishes. Thus $$z\cdot n_\alpha=g(\alpha,\lambda),\qquad
 z\cdot e_\alpha=g_\theta(\alpha,\lambda),$$ which gives $z=\Gamma_\lambda(\alpha)$. The unique-maximum property proves that this parametrization is one-to-one on $\mathbb{T}$; <a href="#phys:normal-parametrization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:normal-parametrization">[phys:normal-parametrization]</a> makes it regular. Each support line has exactly one contact point, so the boundary contains no nontrivial line segment and the body is strictly convex. In fact its curvature is $1/\rho>0$. All these curves are real analytic.

The last inequality in <a href="#phys:support-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:support-inequalities">[phys:support-inequalities]</a> gives, for $0\leq\lambda<\mu\leq\lambda_0$, $$g(\theta,\mu)\leq g(\theta,\lambda)-c_0(\mu-\lambda)
 \quad\hbox{for every }\theta.$$ It follows that $K_\mu$ is contained in the interior of $K_\lambda$, with a uniform positive gap between them. In particular the boundaries are pairwise disjoint, and $\Gamma_\lambda\subset\Omega$ for $\lambda>0$.

The positively oriented tangent at $\Gamma_\lambda(\theta)$ has velocity $e_\theta$ and line equation $z\cdot n_\theta=g(\theta,\lambda)$. For $\lambda>0$ these are precisely the line states in <a href="#phys:invariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:invariance">[phys:invariance]</a>, with $t=\sqrt\lambda$. They meet $\Omega$, because their contact points lie in its interior. The onto invariance in <a href="#phys:invariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phys:invariance">[phys:invariance]</a> therefore preserves tangency under both $T$ and $T^{-1}$. It supplies the tangent successor segment at the forward endpoint and the tangent predecessor segment at the back endpoint. Reflection is a linear involution: if $v'$ is the reflection of $v$, then $-v$ is the reflection of $-v'$. Reversing each such pair of segments proves tangency preservation for the opposite orientation as well. Thus every positive leaf is a smooth closed convex caustic, with the required reflection property at either endpoint.

It remains to show that these curves fill a physical collar. The map $$F:\mathbb{T}\times[0,\lambda_0]\longrightarrow\overline\Omega,
 \qquad F(\theta,\lambda)=\Gamma_\lambda(\theta),$$ is continuous and injective, by simplicity of the curves and strict nesting. Since its domain is compact, it is a homeomorphism onto its image. Set $$A=K_0\setminus K_{\lambda_0}.$$ This is relatively open in $K_0=\overline\Omega$ and contains $\partial\Omega$. All leaves with $\lambda<\lambda_0$ lie in $A$. For the converse, fix $z\in A$ and consider its minimum support slack $$m_z(\lambda)
   =\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}
      \bigl(g(\theta,\lambda)-z\cdot n_\theta\bigr).$$ This function is continuous, satisfies $m_z(0)\geq0>m_z(\lambda_0)$, and obeys $$m_z(\mu)\leq m_z(\lambda)-c_0(\mu-\lambda)
 \qquad(\mu>\lambda).$$ It has exactly one zero in $[0,\lambda_0)$. At that zero, $z\in\partial K_\lambda$, hence $z=\Gamma_\lambda(\theta)$ for exactly one $\theta$. The restriction of $F$ to $\mathbb{T}\times[0,\lambda_0)$ is therefore a homeomorphism onto $A$. Rescaling $\lambda=b\lambda_0$ completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-physical-collar-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-physical-collar</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:collar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:collar-regularity">3</a>.* Theorem <a href="#thm:analytic-boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:analytic-boundary">7</a> proves that the billiard boundary is real analytic. Theorem <a href="#thm:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint">13</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:physical-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:physical-collar">18</a> give the asserted jointly real-analytic family of strictly convex caustics and its full physical collar. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-physical-collar-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-physical-collar</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:physical-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:physical-collar">18</a> gives exactly the continuous caustic collar of (OpenAI 2026, Definition 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)]: the boundary is the zero leaf, the positive leaves are smooth closed convex caustics, and the family parametrizes a relatively open neighborhood of the boundary in the closed table. The original assumptions supply the remaining smoothness, strict convexity, and positive curvature hypotheses. Theorem <a href="#thm:nb-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nb-rigidity">2</a>, namely (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[NB](#ref-NB)], therefore implies that $\Omega$ is an ellipse. Undoing the initial translation gives a center $c\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and a real symmetric positive-definite matrix $Q$ with $$\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^2:(x-c)^TQ(x-c)<1\},$$ as required. ◻

</details>

Positive $g + g_{θθ}$ makes each envelope strictly convex, and $g_{λ}",
      "figure_id": "fig:phase-to-caustic",

          "exact_quote": "The support-envelope correspondence, drawn schematically.\nThe highlighted point of $p=g(\\theta,\\lambda)$ specifies the blue\ntangent line on the right; its contact point is\n$\\Gamma_\\lambda(\\theta_0)=g n_{\\theta_0}+g_\\theta e_{\\theta_0}$,\nwit",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",

\\pgfmathsetmacro{\\contactx}{.85*4.34*cos(60)/\\selectedsupport}\n  \\pgfmathsetmacro{\\contacty}{.85*1.61*sin(60)/\\selectedsupport}\n  \\begin{scope}\n    \\draw[->] (0,0)--(4.55,0) node[right] {$\\theta$};\n    \\draw[->] (0,0)--(0,2.7) node[above] {$p$};\n    \\draw[densely dotted,gray] (4,.3)--(4,2.45);\n    \\draw[thick,domain=-90:270,samples=81,smooth,variable=\\a]\n      plot ({(\\a+90)/90},{sqrt(5.29*cos(\\a)^2+2.56*sin(\\a)^2)});\n    \\draw[blue!65!black,thick,domain=-90:270,samples=81,smooth,variable=\\a]\n      plot ({(\\a+90)/90},{sqrt(4.34*cos(\\a)^2+1.61*sin(\\a)^2)});\n    \\draw[gray,domain=-90:270,samples=81,smooth,variable=\\a]\n      plot ({(\\a+90)/90},{sqrt(3.64*cos(\\a)^2+.91*sin(\\a)^2)});\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (4.08,1.66) {$h$};\n    \\node[anchor=west,blue!65!black] at (4.08,1.25) {$g(\\cdot,\\lambda)$};\n    \\draw[densely dotted] (1.6667,0)--(1.6667,\\selectedsupport);\n    \\fill[blue!65!black] (1.6667,\\selectedsupport) circle (2pt);\n    \\node[below] at (1.6667,0) {$\\theta_0$};\n    \\node at (2,-.63) {analytic invariant line graphs};\n  \\end{scope}\n  \\draw[->,thick] (5.35,1.95)--(6.45,1.95);\n  \\node[align=center,font=\\scriptsize] at (5.9,2.45) {support\\\\envelope};\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=9.15cm,yshift=1.4cm]\n    \\draw[thick] (0,0) ellipse [x radius=1.955,y radius=1.36];\n    \\draw[blue!65!black,thick] (0,0)\n      ellipse [x radius=1.77076,y radius=1.07853];\n    \\draw[gray] (0,0) ellipse [x radius=1.62173,y radius=.81086];\n    \\draw[blue!65!black]\n      ({\\contactx-1.2*cos(150)},{\\contacty-1.2*sin(150)})--\n      ({\\contactx+1.2*cos(150)},{\\contacty+1.2*sin(150)});\n    \\fill[blue!65!black] (\\contactx,\\contacty) circle (2pt);\n    \\draw[gray,thin,densely dotted] (\\contactx,\\contacty)--(2.27,.85);\n    \\node[anchor=west,blue!65!black] at (2.35,.85)\n      {$\\Gamma_\\lambda(\\theta_0)$};\n    \\node[anchor=south,blue!65!black,font=\\scriptsize] at (.85,1.56)\n      {$z\\cdot n_{\\theta_0}=g(\\theta_0,\\lambda)$};\n    \\draw[->] (0,0)--(.34,.59) node[right] {$n_{\\theta_0}$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (1.65,-.93) {$\\partial\\Omega$};\n    \\node at (.1,-2.03) {nested support envelopes};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two schematic diagrams side by side illustrating the support-envelope correspondence:\n- Left diagram (\"analytic invariant line graphs\"): A Cartesian coordinate system with horizontal axis labeled $\\theta$ and vertical axis labeled $p$. Two oscillating curves are plotted: an upper curve labeled $h$ and a lower purple curve labeled $g(\\cdot, \\lambda)$. A point on $g(\\cdot, \\lambda)$ at coordinate $\\theta_0$ is highlighted with a blue dot, with dashed projection lines extending down to $\\theta_0$ on the $\\theta$-axis and left to the $p$-axis.\n- Right diagram (\"nested support envelopes\"): Two nested convex planar curves. The outer closed curve is labeled $\\partial\\Omega$. The inner closed curve is indicated by an arrow as \"support envelope\". A blue tangent line touches the support envelope at a highlighted point labeled $\\Gamma_\\lambda(\\theta_0)$. The line is labeled $z \\cdot n_{\\theta_0} = g(\\theta_0, \\lambda)$. A normal vector arrow perpendicular to the tangent line at the contact point is labeled $n_{\\theta_0}$."
    }
